<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{th: CRT limit if q fixed}
    Fix $q\in(0,1]$.
    For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$ be a $q$-descent-biased random tree of size $n$.
    Let $W_{n,0} := \rho_n$ be the root of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$ and $(W_{n,i})_{i\ge1}$ be a sequence of $\mu_n$-i.i.d.~random vertices of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$.
    Also let $W_{0} := \rho$ be the root of $\mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e}$ and $(W_{i})_{i\ge1}$ be a sequence of $\mu$-i.i.d.~random points on $\mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e}$.
    Then, for any given $\ell\in\mathbb{N}$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \left(\big. \frac{q-1}{\sqrt q \log q} \,\frac{1}{\sqrt n}
        \,\mathrm{dist}_n{\left( W_{n,i} , W_{n,j} \right)} \right)_{0\le i,j\le \ell}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        \left(\big. \mathrm{dist}{\left( W_{i} , W_{j} \right)} \right)_{0\le i,j\le \ell}
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution and with joint moments.
    In particular,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{q-1}{\sqrt q \log q} \frac{1}{\sqrt n} \cdot \mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        \mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e}
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution for the Gromov--Prokhorov topology.
    When $q=1$, we interpret the factor $\sqrt q \frac{\log q}{q-1}$ as $1$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{th: dendron limit if a/n}
    Fix $a>0$.
    For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let $q_n=a/n$ and let $\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}$ be a $q_n$-descent-biased random tree of size $n$.
    There exists an explicit random dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{1}{\log n} \cdot \mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        \mathcal{D}_a
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution for the dendron topology.
    The dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$ is constructed in \Cref{def: size-biased dendron}.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{th: our dendron cv to CRT}
    The dendrons $\mathcal{D}_a$, $a \in \mathbb{R}_+^*$, interpolate between the trivial dendron $\Upsilon_{\delta_1}$ and the CRT $\mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e}$. 
    More precisely, in the sense of dendrons, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        (i)\quad 
        \mathcal{D}_a \,\underset{a\to0}{\longrightarrow}\, \Upsilon_{\delta_1} 
        \qquad\text{and}\qquad
        (ii)\quad 
        \frac{1}{\sqrt a} \cdot \mathcal{D}_a  \,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, \mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e}
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:bs local limit}
Let $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{n \geq 1}$ be a sequence of random finite trees, and let $u_n$ denote a uniformly random vertex in $\mathcal{T}_n$. 
Let $(\mathcal{T}_*,\rho_*)$ be a random rooted --- possibly infinite --- locally finite tree. 
We say that $\mathcal{T}_n$ converges locally to $(\mathcal{T}_*,\rho_*)$ if, for every fixed $r \geq 0$, we have the convergence in distribution
\begin{align*}
B_r(\mathcal{T}_n,u_n) \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} B_r(\mathcal{T}_*,\rho_*) \,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:grafted trees}
    For any $q\in[0,1]$ and $\chi \in [0,1]$, define a random variable $Y_{q,\chi}$ taking values in $\mathbb{N}^2$, such that for all $m \geq i \geq 1$,
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(Y_{q,\chi} = (m,i)\right)} = f_{m,i}^{(q)}(\chi)
    \end{align*}
    where the function $f_{m,i}^{(q)}$ is defined later by \eqref{eq: function fmi}.
    For all $M\ge i \ge 1$, let $\mathcal{T}^{(q)}_{M,i}$ denote a $q$-descent-biased tree conditioned to have size $M$ and root label $i$. 
    Finally, define the (a.s.\ finite) random tree $\mathcal{T}^{(q)}_\chi$ as the mixture of conditioned $q$-descent-biased trees:
    \begin{align*}
    \mathcal{L}{\left(\left.\mathcal{T}^{(q)}_\chi  \,\right\vert\,  Y_{q,\chi}=(M,i) \right)} = \mathcal{L}{\left( \mathcal{T}^{(q)}_{M,i} \right)} \,.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:t etoile q}
    For $q \in [0,1]$, consider the kernel 
    \[
        \kappa_q(\mathrm dy|x)=\frac{1}{x+q(1-x)} \mathbbm{1}_{y \in [0,x]} \mathrm dy + \frac{q}{x+q(1-x)} \mathbbm{1}_{y \in [x,1]} \mathrm dy \,.
    \]
    Let $(X_k)_{k \geq 0}$ be the Markov chain on $[0,1]$ with Markov kernel $\kappa_q$, started at a uniform variable $X_0 \sim \mathrm{Unif}{\left([0,1]\right)}$. 
    We define the tree $\mathcal{T}_*^{(q)}$ as the random discrete infinite tree obtained by starting from a infinite spine $(v_0,v_1,\ldots)$, rooted at $\rho_*^{(q)} := v_0$, and grafting an independent copy of $\mathcal{T}_{X_i}^{(q)}$ onto $v_i$ (identifying the root of $\mathcal{T}_{X_i}^{(q)}$ with $v_i$, and forgetting the labels).
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rk:kernels}
    The Markov chain $(X_k)_{k\ge0}$ intuitively describes the rescaled labels of the first vertices on the path from a uniform vertex to the root.
    For $q=0$, 
                                                                          
    the law of $X_{k+1}$ given $X_k$ is uniform on $[0,X_k]$, and given the Markov chain $(X_k)_{k\ge0}$, the grafted trees $\mathcal{T}_{X_k}^{(0)}$ are independent random recursive trees with independent $\mathrm{Geom}(X_k)$ sizes.
    For $q=1$, the sequence $(X_k)_{k\ge0}$ is i.i.d.~uniform,
                                                                                                                 
                                                                                                                           
    and the grafted trees $\mathcal{T}_{X_k}^{(1)}$ are i.i.d.\ random Cayley trees with i.i.d.\ random sizes given by the probability mass function $m\in\mathbb{N} \mapsto e^{-m} m^{m-1} / m!$.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{th: BS local limit unified}
    Let $(q_n) \in [0,1]^\mathbb{N}$ be a sequence such that $q_* := \lim_{n \to \infty} q_n \in [0,1]$ exists.
    Then, $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q_n)}$ converges locally in the Benjamini--Schramm sense to the random rooted tree $(\mathcal{T}_*^{(q_*)},\rho_*^{(q_*)})$.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rk:root local limit}
    The second and third authors used a slightly different notion of local convergence in \cite{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}, and proved the convergence in distribution of $B_r(\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}, \rho_n)$ for constant $q$, where $\rho_n$ is the root of the $q$-descent-biased tree $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
In this work, we only deal with the Gromov--Prokhorov topology.
In the context of real trees, however, it is also common to consider the \emph{Gromov--Hausdorff} (GH) topology.
Together with the GP topology, these combine into the stronger \emph{Gromov--Hausdorff--Prokhorov} (GHP) topology.
Extending the convergence of \Cref{th: CRT limit if q fixed} to the GHP topology was out of reach for us, and this remains an open question.
The main challenge lies in establishing bounds on global statistics, such as height, of descent-biased trees.
Note that this question would not make sense in the context of \Cref{th: dendron limit if a/n}, as there is no suitable extension of the GH topology to the space of dendrons.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
We believe that similar results could be obtained across the entire $q_n \to0$, $nq_n\to\infty$ range, although this might require heavier computation.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:trees with smallest root}
    Let $\mathcal{E}_{1}$ denote the set of trees $t \in \mathbb{T}$ with root labeled $1$, and
    \begin{align*}
        A^{(1)}(x,q) := \sum_{t\in \mathbb{T}} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t\right)}} \frac{x^{|t|}}{|t|!}  \mathbbm{1}_{t \in \mathcal{E}_1}
    \end{align*}
    denote the (exponential) generating function of descent-biased trees in $\mathcal{E}_1$.
    Then, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \partial_x A^{(1)}(x,q) = e^{A(x,q)}. 
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
This is a straightforward application of our decomposition.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem: generating function typical depth}
    For a tree $t\in \mathbb{T}$, recall that $\rho(t)$ denotes its root and $\mathrm{dist}$ denotes its graph metric. Let
    \begin{equation*}
        F_1(x,y,q) := \sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t\right)}} \frac{x^{\left|t\right|}}{{\left|t\right|}!} \sum_{w\in t} y^{\mathrm{dist}(\rho(t), w)}
    \end{equation*}
    be the generating function of the depth of a typical vertex in a descent-biased tree.
    Then, we have the differential equation
    \begin{align}\label{eq: equadiff on F1}
        \partial_x F_1(x,y,q) 
        = \partial_x A(x,q) \left(
        1 + (1+q)y F_1(x,y,q) + qy^2 F_1(x,y,q)^2 
        \right) \,,
    \end{align}
    with the solution
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: explicit formula for generating function of typical depth}
        F_1(x,y,q) = \frac{ e^{(1-q)yA(x,q)} - 1 }{ y - qye^{(1-q)yA(x,q)} } \,.
    \end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
For $t\in\mathbb{T}$, write 
    \[
        G(t,y) := \sum_{w\in t} y^{\mathrm{dist}(\rho(t), w)} \,.
    \]
    Consider a tree $t\in\mathbb{T}_{n+1}$, let $v_1$ denote its vertex labeled $1$, let $p\ge0$ be the number of children of $v_1$, and let $t_1, \dots, t_p$ be the subtrees of $t$ rooted at the children of $v_1$ (ordered in an arbitrary manner).
                                                 
    If $v_1 = \rho(t)$, then
    \[
        G(t,y) = 1 + y \sum_{i=1}^p G(t_i,y) \,.
    \]
    If $v_1 \ne \rho(t)$, then we let $t_0$ denote the tree $t$ minus $v_1$ and its descendants, let $t_1, \dots, t_p$ be the subtrees rooted at the children of $v_1$, and we let $v_0\in t_0$ denote the parent of $v_1$ in $t$.
    In that case,
    \[
        G(t,y) = G(t_0,y) + y^{1+\mathrm{dist}(\rho(t_0), v_0)} \left( 1 + y \sum_{i=1}^p G(t_i,y) \right) \,.
    \]
    From this analysis, we deduce that
    \begin{align*}
        \partial_x F_1(x,y,q)
        &= \sum_{n\ge0} \sum_{t\in \mathbb{T}_{n+1}} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t\right)}} \frac{x^n}{n!} G(t,y)
        \\&= \sum_{p\ge0} \sum_{t_1, \dots, t_p\in \mathbb{T}} 
        \frac{1}{p!} \binom{{\left|t_1\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|}}{{\left|t_1\right|}, \dots, {\left|t_p\right|}}
        q^{{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_1\right)}}+\dots+{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_p\right)}}} \frac{x^{{\left|t_1\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|}}}{({\left|t_1\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|})!}
        \left( 1 + y \sum_{i=1}^p G(t_i,y) \right)
        \\&\hspace{1em} + \sum_{p\ge0} \sum_{t_0,t_1, \dots, t_p\in \mathbb{T}} \sum_{v_0\in t_0}
        \frac{1}{p!} \binom{{\left|t_0\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|}}{{\left|t_0\right|}, \dots, {\left|t_p\right|}}
        q^{1+{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_0\right)}}+\dots+{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_p\right)}}} \frac{x^{{\left|t_0\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|}}}{({\left|t_0\right|}+\dots+{\left|t_p\right|})!}
        \\&\hspace{11em} \cdot \left( G(t_0,y) + y^{1+\mathrm{dist}(\rho(t_0), v_0)} \left( 1 + y \sum_{i=1}^p G(t_i,y) \right) \right)
        \\&= \sum_{p\ge0} \frac{1}{p!} \left( 
        A(x,q)^p + y p F_1(x,y,q) A(x,q)^{p-1}
        \right)
        \\&\hspace{1em} + q \sum_{p\ge0} \frac{1}{p!} \left(
        x \partial_x F_1(x,y,q) A(x,q)^p + y F_1(x,y,q) A(x,q)^p + y^2 p F_1(x,y,q)^2 A(x,q)^{p-1}
        \right) \,,
    \end{align*}
    and thus
    \begin{equation*}
        e^{-A(x,q)} \partial_x F_1(x,y,q) = 
        1
        + y F_1(x,y,q) 
        + q x \partial_x F_1(x,y,q) 
        + q y F_1(x,y,q) 
        + q y^2 F_1(x,y,q)^2 \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Regrouping the terms containing $\partial_x F_1(x,y,q)$, multiplying by $\partial_x A(x,q)$, and recalling \eqref{eq: differential equation for A}, we get \eqref{eq: equadiff on F1}.
    Observing that this is a Riccati equation, \eqref{eq: explicit formula for generating function of typical depth} follows by standard methods.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop: Rayleigh limit of typical depth when q fixed}
    Recall the function $F_1$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: generating function typical depth}.
    For $r\in\mathbb{N}_0$, we let $f_{1,r}(x,q)$ denote the $r$-th derivative of $F_1(x,y,q)$ with respect to $y$, evaluated at $y=1$.
    Then, for fixed $q\in(0,1]$, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: claim singularity f1r}
        f_{1,r}(x,q) \sim \frac{r!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^r \left( 1 - \frac{x}{x_0(q)} \right)^{-1/2-r/2}
    \end{equation}
    as $x\to x_0(q)$.
    As a consequence, if $u_n$ denotes a uniformly random vertex of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$ and $\mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n)$ its depth, then
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{q-1}{\sqrt{q} \log q} \frac{1}{\sqrt n} \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) 
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \mathrm{Rayleigh}(1)
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution and with convergence of all moments as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let us skip the dependence on $q$ in notation.
    We first prove \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r} by induction on $r$.
    For $r=0$, we simply have $f_{1,0}(x) = x\partial_x A(x)$.
    By known closure properties of functions amenable to singularity analysis \cite[Section VI.10]{Flajolet_Sedgewick_2009}, we can differentiate~\eqref{eq: asymptotic A close to singularity for fixed q} to get
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: singularity for single derivative of A and for f10}
    \partial_x A(x) = \frac{1}{x_0\sqrt{2q}} (1-x/x_0)^{-1/2} + \mathcal{O}(1) ,\quad
    \text{ and thus,}\quad
    f_{1,0}(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2q}} (1-x/x_0)^{-1/2} + \mathcal{O}(1)    
    \end{equation}
    as $x\to x_0$.
    For $r=1$, observe that $f_{1,1}(x)$ is the generating function for the mean path length of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$.
    It is thus the function $B$ of \cite{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}, for which \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r} was proved in \cite[above Proposition~25]{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}.
    Now consider $r\ge2$, and assume that \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r} holds for all orders up to $r-1$.
    Let us differentiate equation \eqref{eq: equadiff on F1} of Lemma~\ref{lem: generating function typical depth} a total of $r$ times with respect to $y$:
    \begin{align*}
        \partial_x \partial_y^r F_1(x,y) = \partial_x A(x) \Bigg(
        &(1+q)y \partial_y^r F_1(x,y) 
        + r(1+q) \partial_y^{r-1} F_1(x,y) 
        + qy^2\sum_{k=0}^r \binom{r}{k} \partial_y^k F_1(x,y) \partial_y^{r-k} F_1(x,y) 
        \\&+ 2rqy\sum_{k=0}^{r-1} \binom{r-1}{k} \partial_y^k F_1(x,y) \partial_y^{r-1-k} F_1(x,y) 
        \\&+ r(r-1)q\sum_{k=0}^{r-2} \binom{r-2}{k} \partial_y^k F_1(x,y) \partial_y^{r-2-k} F_1(x,y)
        \Bigg) \,.
    \end{align*}
    At $y=1$, this becomes
    \begin{align*}
        \partial_x f_{1,r}(x) = \partial_x A(x) \Bigg(
        &(1+q) f_{1,r}(x) 
        + r(1+q) f_{1,r-1}(x) 
        + q\sum_{k=0}^r \binom{r}{k} f_{1,k}(x) f_{1,r-k}(x) 
        \\&+ 2rq\sum_{k=0}^{r-1} \binom{r-1}{k} f_{1,k}(x) f_{1,r-1-k}(x) 
        + r(r-1)q\sum_{k=0}^{r-2} \binom{r-2}{k} f_{1,k}(x) f_{1,r-2-k}(x)
        \Bigg) \,.
    \end{align*}
    Isolating the terms containing $f_{1,r}$ on the right-hand side, we can write 
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_x f_{1,r}(x) = f_{1,r}(x) G_1'(x) + R_{1,r}(x)
    \end{equation*}
    where $G_1'(x) := \partial_x A(x) \left(\big. (1+q) + 2qf_{1,0}(x) \right)$, and $R_{1,r}$ contains only $f_{1,\alpha}$'s with $\alpha<r$.
    The function $G_1$ is defined as an appropriate primitive of $G_1'$, to be specified later.
    We solve this linear ODE using variation of parameters to obtain
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: solution f1r with variation of constant}
        f_{1,r}(x) = e^{G_1(x)} \int_0^x R_{1,r}(u) e^{-G_1(u)} \mathrm{d}u \,.
    \end{equation}
    From \eqref{eq: singularity for single derivative of A and for f10}, we get
    \begin{equation*}
        G_1'(x) = \frac{1}{x_0} (1-x/x_0)^{-1} + \mathcal{O}\left( {\left|x-x_0\right|}^{-1/2} \right) ,
        \quad\text{and thus}\quad
        G_1(x) = -\log(1-x/x_0) + \mathcal{O}\left( {\left|x-x_0\right|}^{1/2} \right)
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$, for a unique adequate choice of $G_1$.
    This implies
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: singularity for expG1}
        e^{G_1(x)} \sim (1-x/x_0)^{-1} 
    \end{equation}
    as $x\to x_0$.
    Next, using the induction hypothesis and the fact that $r\ge2$, it is straightforward to find the dominant terms inside $R_{1,r}$:
    \begin{equation*}
        R_{1,r}(x) 
        \;\sim\; q \partial_x A(x)
        \sum_{k=1}^{r-1} \binom{r}{k} f_{1,k}(x) f_{1,r-k}(x)
        \;\sim\; \frac{r!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^r \frac{r-1}{2x_0} (1-x/x_0)^{-3/2 - r/2}
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$.
    Therefore, with \eqref{eq: singularity for expG1},
    \begin{equation*}
        \int_0^x R_{1,r}(u) e^{-G_1(u)} \mathrm{d}u 
        \;\sim\; \frac{r!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^r (1-x/x_0)^{1/2 - r/2} \,,
    \end{equation*}
    and thus, using \eqref{eq: solution f1r with variation of constant},
    \begin{equation*}
        f_{1,r}(x) \sim \frac{r!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^r (1-x/x_0)^{-1/2 - r/2}
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$.
    This completes the proof of \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r}.
    Now, for the second claim, observe that
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ \left( \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) \right)_r \right]} = \frac{[x^n] f_{1,r}(x)}{n [x^n] A(x)} \,,
    \end{align*}
    where $(.)_r$ denotes the $r$-th falling factorial.
    Using \eqref{eq: asymptotic coefficient A for fixed q}, \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r} and singularity analysis, we get
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ \left( \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) \right)_r \right]} 
        \;\sim\; \frac{r! \sqrt\pi}{\Gamma(1/2+r/2)} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^r n^{r/2}
    \end{align*}
    as $n\to\infty$.
    Therefore, the same holds for the expectation of the $r$-th power of $\mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n)$, and thus
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ \left( \frac{q-1}{\sqrt q \log q} \frac{1}{\sqrt n} \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) \right)^r \right]} 
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \frac{r! \sqrt\pi}{2^{r/2}\Gamma(1/2+r/2)} 
        = 2^{r/2} \Gamma(1+r/2)
    \end{align*}
    as $n\to\infty$, using Legendre's duplication formula.
    The right-hand side represents the moments of a standard Rayleigh distribution.
    Since this distribution is characterized by its moments, this concludes the proof of the proposition.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor: CLT descents q fixed}
    Let $q\in(0,1]$ be fixed.
    Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{{\mathrm{des}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}\right)}} - \mu(q) n}{\sqrt{\nu(q) n}}
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\,
        \mathcal{N}{\left(0,1\right)}
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution as $n\to\infty$, where $\mu(q) = \frac{q}{q-1}\left( 1 - \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)$ and $\nu(q) = \frac{q(1+q)\log q}{(q-1)^3} -\frac{2q}{(q-1)^2}$ (for $q=1$, these are to be interpreted as their respective limits $\frac12$ and $\frac16$).
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Uniformly for $u$ in a (real) neighborhood of $1$, we have (by \eqref{eq: asymptotic coefficient A for fixed q})
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[u^{{\mathrm{des}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}\right)}}}\right]}
        = \frac{[x^n] A(x,qu)}{[x^n] A(x,q)}
        \sim \frac{1}{\sqrt u} \left( \frac{x_0(qu)}{x_0(q)} \right)^{-n}
        = e^{n\cdot \alpha(u) + \beta(u)} ( 1 + o(1) )
    \end{align*}
    as $n\to\infty$, where $\alpha(u) = q\frac{\log q}{1-q} - qu\frac{\log qu}{1-qu}$ and $\beta(u) = -\frac{1}{2}\log u$.
    The result follows from a standard application of Curtiss's theorem \cite{Curtiss_1942}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}
    Fix $M>0$.
    Then, uniformly for $a\in[0,M]$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] A(x,a/n) \sim \frac{n^{a-1}}{e^a \Gamma(a+1)}
    \end{equation*}
    as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic coefficient exponentials in transition regime}
    Fix $M>0$ and $j\ge0$.
    Then, uniformly for $a\in[0,M]$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] e^{j A(x,a/n)} \sim  \frac{j n^{a+j-1}}{e^{a} \Gamma(a+j+1)}
    \end{equation*}
    as $n\to\infty$.
    As a consequence, the probability that the root of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}$ has label $1$ is asymptotically equal to $\frac{1}{a+1}$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: asymptotic coefficient typical depth in transition regime}
    Recall the function $F_1$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: generating function typical depth}.
    For any fixed $a\ge0$ and $y\in(0,1]$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] F_1{\left( x, y^{1/\log n}, a/n \right)} \sim n^{a} e^{-a} \sum_{k\ge1} k a^{k-1} y^{k} \frac{1}{\Gamma(a+k+1)}
    \end{equation*}
    as $n\to\infty$.
    As a consequence, if $u_n$ denotes a uniformly random vertex of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}$, then $\frac{1}{\log n} \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n)$ converges in distribution to a discrete random variable with probability mass function $k\mapsto \frac{k a^{k-1}}{(a+k)\cdots(a+1)}$ on $\mathbb{N}$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
For $q \in (0,1)$, recall that $x\mapsto A(x,q)$ is analytic on $\mathbb{D}_A := \mathbb{D}(0,x_0(q)+\delta) \setminus \mathbb{R}_{\ge x_0(q)}$ for some $\delta>0$, where $x_0 = x_0(q):= q^{q/(1-q)}$.
    On this domain, it is one-to-one with inverse $z\mapsto \frac{e^{-qz}-e^{-z}}{1-q} = A^{-1}(z,q)$.
    Also, $A(0,q)=0$, and from \eqref{eq: asymptotic A close to singularity for fixed q}, $A(x_0,q) = \frac{\log q}{q-1} \in [0,\infty]$.
    
    Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a simple closed contour in $\mathbb{D}_A \setminus \{0\}$ that winds around $0$ in counterclockwise direction (for example, a sufficiently small circle centered at $0$).
    By Cauchy's integral formula, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] A(x,q) = \frac{1}{n}[x^{n-1}] \partial_x A(x,q) 
        = \frac{1}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_\mathcal{C} \frac{1}{x^n} \partial_x A(x,q) \mathrm{d}x \,.
    \end{equation*}
    The change of variables $z = A(x,q)$, $\mathrm{d}z = \partial_x A \cdot \mathrm{d}x$, $x = \frac{e^{-qz}-e^{-z}}{1-q}$, yields
    \begin{align*}
        [x^n] A(x,q)
        = \frac{(1-q)^n}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{C}'} \left( e^{-qz} - e^{-z} \right)^{-n} \mathrm{d}z\,,
    \end{align*}
    where $\mathcal{C}' := A(\mathcal{C},q)$ is a simple contour in $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{0\}$ that winds around $0$ in counterclockwise direction.
    Using the standard determination of $\log$, the map $z\mapsto ne^{-z}$ is one-to-one from the strip $\left\{ z \in \mathbb{C}, \, {\left|\Im(z)\right|} < \pi \right\}$ onto $\mathbb{C} \setminus \mathbb{R}_{\le0}$. If $\mathcal{C}$ is taken to be sufficiently small, then $\mathcal{C}'$ lies entirely inside this strip and in particular avoids all singularities of the integrand (which are located at $z = \frac{2ki\pi}{1-q}$, $k \in \mathbb{Z}$) except for $0$.    
    Now set $q=a/n$ and use the further change of variables $u = ne^{-z}$, $z = -\log(u/n)$, $\mathrm{d}z = -\frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}$ to obtain
    \begin{align}\label{eq: Cauchy contour formula for A after two changes of variables in transition regime}
        [x^n] A(x,a/n)
        = \frac{-(1-a/n)^n}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{C}''} \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n} \frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u},
    \end{align}
    where $\mathcal{C}'' := n\exp(-\mathcal{C}')$ is a simple contour in $\mathbb{C} \setminus \mathbb{R}_{\le0}$, that winds around $n$ in clockwise direction.
    By analyticity, we can use any such contour without changing the value of the above integral.
    Specifically, we take $\mathcal{C}'':= \gamma_\text{small} \cup \gamma_\text{long,1} \cup \gamma_\text{big} \cup \gamma_\text{long,2}$, where
    \begin{align}\label{eq: contour left-pacman}
        \begin{array}{llll}
        &\gamma_\text{small} 
        &:= &\left\{ u = r e^{i\varphi} : -\frac\pi2 \le \varphi \le \frac\pi2 \right\} ;\vspace{.5em}
        \\&\gamma_\text{long,1} 
        &:= &\left\{ u = -t + r i : 0\le t\le n^2 \right\} ;\vspace{.5em}
        \\&\gamma_\text{big} 
        &:= &\left\{ u = \sqrt{n^4 + r^2} e^{i\varphi} : {\left|\varphi\right|} \le \arccos\left(-\sqrt{n^4/(n^4+r^2)}\right) \right\} ;\vspace{.5em}
        \\&\gamma_\text{long,2} 
        &:= &\left\{ u = -t - r i : n^2\ge t\ge 0 \right\} ;
        \end{array}
    \end{align}
    and $r > 0$ is fixed and arbitrary, see \Cref{fig: contour left-pacman}.
    With this, we decompose \eqref{eq: Cauchy contour formula for A after two changes of variables in transition regime} into
    
    \begin{figure}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=0.4\linewidth]{contour_left_pacman.pdf}
        \caption{The contour described by \eqref{eq: contour left-pacman}.}
        \label{fig: contour left-pacman}
    \end{figure}
    
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: Cauchy formula for A into 4 parts}
        [x^n] A(x,a/n) = \frac{-(1-a/n)^n}{n} \left( I_\text{small} + I_\text{long,1} + I_\text{big} + I_\text{long,2} \right)
    \end{equation}
    according to the part of the contour we integrate on.
    Let us analyze the term appearing inside the integral of \eqref{eq: Cauchy contour formula for A after two changes of variables in transition regime}.
    All points $u$ on $\mathcal{C}''$ satisfy $r^2\le {\left|u\right|}^2 \le n^4+r^2$, so we can write 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: expansion non-inverse integrand before power n}
        \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} 
        = 1 + \frac{a}{n}\log\left(\frac{u}{n}\right) - \frac{u}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log^2n}{n^2}\right)\,,
    \end{equation}
    where the $\mathcal{O}$ is uniform in $u\in\mathcal{C}''$ and in $a\in[0,M]$.
    From now on, the analysis differs for each part of the contour.
    To this aim, choose a sequence $m_n$ such that $m_n = o(\sqrt n)$ and $\log n = o(m_n)$.
    
    \paragraph{Small $u$'s.}
    Assume that $r\le |u|\le m_n$.
    From \eqref{eq: expansion non-inverse integrand before power n}, we derive that
    \begin{equation*}
        \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-1}
        = 1 - \frac{a}{n}\log\left(\frac{u}{n}\right) + \frac{u}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n^2}\right)
        =: 1 + \frac{w}{n},
    \end{equation*}
    where $w = w(u)$ depends on $u$ and the error is uniform for $r\le |u|\le m_n$ and $a\in[0,M]$.
    With the same uniformity, we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        e^w = \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{-a} e^u \left( 1 + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n}\right) \right)
    \end{equation*}
    and
    \begin{align*}
        \left( 1 + \frac{w}{n} \right)^n
        = e^{n\log\left(1+\frac{w}{n}\right)}
        = e^{n\left(\frac{w}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|w|^2}{n^2}\right)\right)}
        = e^w \left( 1 + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n}\right) \right) \,.
    \end{align*}
    Combining those three, we get
    \begin{align}\label{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate small u}
        \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n}
        = n^a u^{-a} e^u  \left( 1 + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n}\right) \right)\,,
    \end{align}
    uniformly for $r\le |u|\le m_n$ and $a\in[0,M]$.
    
    \paragraph{Medium $u$'s.}
    Assume that $u = -t \pm ri$ with $\sqrt{m_n^2-r^2}\le t\le n^2$. 
    Then, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        {\left| 1 + \frac{a}{n}\log\left(\frac{u}{n}\right) - \frac{u}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log^2n}{n^2}\right) \right|}
        = {\left| 1 + \frac{t}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right) \right|}
        \ge 1 + \frac{m_n}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)\,,
    \end{equation*}
    uniformly for $t \in [\sqrt{m_n^2-r^2},n^2]$ and $a\in[0,M]$.
    Therefore, with the same uniformity,
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate medium u}
        {\left| \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right|}^{-n}
        \le \left( 1 + \frac{m_n}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right) \right)^{-n}
        = e^{-n\log\left( 1 + \frac{m_n}{n} + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right) \right)}
        = \mathcal{O}\left( e^{-m_n(1+o(1))} \right) \,.
    \end{equation}
    
    \paragraph{Large $u$'s.}
    If ${\left|u\right|}^2 = n^4+r^2$, then
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate large u}
       {\left| \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n} \frac1u \right|}
        \le n^{-n+\mathcal{O}(1)}\,,
    \end{equation}
    uniformly for $u\in\gamma_\text{big}$ and $a\in[0,M]$.

    \paragraph{Transfer of the estimates.}
    Now, we can transfer these estimates to the integrals in \eqref{eq: Cauchy formula for A into 4 parts}.
    All $\mathcal{O}$'s below hold uniformly for $a\in[0,M]$.
    First, using \eqref{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate small u} on the small half-circle, we get
    \begin{align*}
        I_\text{small} 
        = \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\gamma_\text{small}} \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n} \frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}
        = \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\gamma_\text{small}} n^{a} u^{-a-1} e^u \mathrm{d}u\,\cdot \left( 1 + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n}\right) \right) \,.
    \end{align*}
    On $\gamma_\text{long,1}$, we can cut the path at $t_n:=\sqrt{m_n^2-r^2}$ and apply \Cref{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate small u,eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate medium u} to the two different parts to get
    \begin{equation*}
        I_\text{long,1} 
        = - \frac{1}{2i\pi} \int_0^{t_n} n^{a} (-t+ri)^{-a-1} e^{-t+ri} \mathrm{d}t \,\cdot \left( 1 + \mathcal{O}\left(\frac{m_n^2}{n}\right) \right)
        + \mathcal{O}\left( e^{-m_n(1+o(1))} \right) \,,
    \end{equation*}
    and further
    \begin{equation*}
        I_\text{long,1} 
        = - \frac{1}{2i\pi} \int_0^{\infty} n^{a} (-t+ri)^{-a-1} e^{-t+ri} \mathrm{d}t \,\cdot \left( 1 + o(1) \right) \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Likewise, for $\gamma_\text{long,2}$,
    \begin{equation*}
        I_\text{long,2} 
        = \frac{1}{2i\pi} \int_0^{\infty} n^{a} (-t-ri)^{-a-1} e^{-t-ri} \mathrm{d}t \,\cdot \left( 1 + o(1) \right) \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Finally, on $\gamma_\text{big}$ we can use \eqref{eq: Hankel computation of A - uniform estimate large u} to obtain the bound
    \begin{equation*}
        {\left|I_\text{big}\right|} \le n^{-n+\mathcal{O}(1)} \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Coming back to \eqref{eq: Cauchy formula for A into 4 parts}, we deduce that
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] A(x,a/n) = n^{a-1} \frac{e^{-a}}{2i\pi} \oint_\mathcal{H} u^{-a-1} e^u \mathrm{d}u\,\cdot \left( 1 + o(1) \right)
    \end{equation*}
    where
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: Hankel contour}
        \mathcal{H} = \left\{ u = -t+ r i : t\ge0 \right\}
        \cup \left\{ u = r e^{i\varphi} : -\tfrac\pi2\le \varphi\le \tfrac{\pi}2 \right\}
        \cup \left\{ u = -t- r i : 0\le t \right\}
    \end{equation}
    is a positive Hankel contour (i.e., traversed clockwise).
    We can finally use the Hankel form of the Gamma function, i.e.,
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{1}{\Gamma(\alpha)} = \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{H}} u^{-\alpha} e^u \mathrm{d}u\,,
    \end{equation*}
    which is valid for all $\alpha\in\mathbb{C}$, to conclude the proof.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The approach is largely the same as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}.
    Let $\mathcal{C}$ be the same contour around $0$ as in that proof.
    By Cauchy's integral formula, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n] e^{jA(x,q)} = \frac{j}{n}[x^{n-1}] e^{jA(x,q)} \partial_x A(x,q) 
        = \frac{j}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_\mathcal{C} \frac{e^{jA(x,q)}}{x^n} \partial_x A(x,q) \mathrm{d}x \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Applying the same change of variables (namely, $A(x,q) = z = - \log(u,n)$) as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}, we reach 
    \begin{align*}
        [x^n] e^{jA(x,a/n)}
        = \frac{-j(1-a/n)^n}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{C}''} \left(\frac{n}{u}\right)^j \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n} \frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}\,,
    \end{align*}
    where $\mathcal{C}''$ is again the contour defined in~\eqref{eq: contour left-pacman}.
    The same bounds lead to
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n]e^{jA(x,a/n)} = n^{a+j-1} \frac{je^{-a}}{2i\pi} \oint_\mathcal{H} u^{-a-j-1} e^u \mathrm{d}u\,\cdot \left( 1 + o(1) \right) \,,
    \end{equation*}
    which yields the stated formula.
    For the second claim, simply note that
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbf{P}{\left( \mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)} \in \mathcal{E}_1 \right)}
        = \frac{[x^n] A^{(1)}(x,a/n)}{[x^n] A(x,a/n)}
        = \frac{1}{n} \frac{[x^{n-1}] e^{A(x,a/n)}}{[x^n] A(x,a/n)}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, \frac{1}{a+1}
    \end{equation*}
    by Lemma~\ref{lem:trees with smallest root}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Fix $y>0$ and set $\nu_n := y^{1/\log n}$.
    By \eqref{eq: explicit formula for generating function of typical depth}, we can write $F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right) = H_n(A(x,a/n))$, where
    \begin{equation*}
        H_n(z) = \frac{1}{\nu_n}\frac{e^{(1-a/n)z\nu_n} - 1}{1 - (a/n)e^{(1-a/n)z\nu_n}} \,.
    \end{equation*}
    This function is analytic on $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{\frac{\log(n/a) + 2ki\pi}{\nu_n(1-a/n)}\,:\, k \in \mathbb{Z}\}$, and
    \begin{equation*}
        H_n'(z) = \left(1-\frac{a}{n}\right)^2 \frac{e^{(1-a/n)z\nu_n}}{\left( 1-(a/n)e^{(1-a/n)z\nu_n} \right)^2} \,.
    \end{equation*}
    As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}, we can write
    \begin{align*}
        [x^n] F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right)
        = \frac{(1-a/n)^n}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{C}'} \frac{H_n'(z)}{\left( e^{-az/n} - e^{-z} \right)^n} \mathrm{d}z\,,
    \end{align*}
    where $\mathcal{C}'$ is a simple contour in $\mathbb{C} \setminus \{0\}$ that winds around $0$ in counterclockwise direction. It also needs to be small enough to avoid $\frac{\log(n/a)}{\nu_n(1-a/n)}$, the smallest singularity of $H_n$.
    We want to perform the change of variables $u = ne^{-z}$ again. Now note that
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\log(n/a)}{\nu_n(1-a/n)} = \frac{\log n - \log a}{1 + \frac{\log y}{\log n} + \mathcal{O}((\log n)^{-2})} = \log n - \log (ay) + \mathcal{O}\big((\log n)^{-1} \big),
    \end{equation*}
    so the image of the singularity $\frac{\log(n/a)}{\nu_n(1-a/n)}$ becomes $ne^{-\log n + \log(ay)} = ay$ in the limit as $n\to\infty$. Hence, for large enough $n$,
    \begin{align*}
        [x^n] F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right)
        \sim \frac{-e^{-a}}{n} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{C}''} H_n'\left(-\log(u/n)\right) \left( \left(\frac{u}{n}\right)^{a/n} - \frac{u}{n} \right)^{-n} \frac{\mathrm{d}u}{u}\,,
    \end{align*}
    where $\mathcal{C}''$ can be chosen as the contour $\gamma_\text{small}  \cup \gamma_\text{long,1} \cup \gamma_\text{big} \cup \gamma_\text{long,2}$ of \eqref{eq: contour left-pacman}, with $r$ taken to be larger than $ay$.
    Using the same bounds as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}, together with the asymptotic formula
    \begin{equation*}
        H_n'(-\log(u/n)) \sim n \frac{yu}{(u-ay)^2}
    \end{equation*}
    for fixed $u$, we get
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n]F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right) \sim n^a \frac{e^{-a}}{2i\pi} \oint_\mathcal{H} \frac{y}{(u-ay)^2} u^{-a} e^u \mathrm{d}u\,,
    \end{equation*}
    where $\mathcal{H}$ is the Hankel contour of \eqref{eq: Hankel contour} with $r>ay$.
    On this contour, we can expand
    \begin{equation*}
        (u-ay)^{-2} = u^{-2} \sum_{k\ge0} (k+1) (ay/u)^k \,.
    \end{equation*}
    The same expansion holds when $u$ is replaced by $|u|$, so Fubini's theorem applies and we deduce
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^n]F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right) \sim n^a e^{-a} \sum_{k\ge0} (k+1) a^k y^{k+1} \frac{1}{2i\pi} \oint_{\mathcal{H}} u^{-a-k-2} e^u \mathrm{d}u
        = n^a e^{-a} \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{ k a^{k-1} y^{k} }{\Gamma(a+k+1)}\,,
    \end{equation*}
    as claimed.
    Finally, using Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}, we obtain
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[\frac1n \sum_{v\in \mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}} y^{\mathrm{dist}(\rho,v)/\log n}\right]}
        = \frac{[x^n] F_1\left(x, \nu_n, a/n\right)}{n [x^n] A(x,a/n)}
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{\Gamma(a+1)}{\Gamma(a+k+1)} k a^{k-1} y^{k} 
    \end{equation*}
    as $n\to\infty$.
    This yields the moment generating function of $\mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) / \log n$, where $u_n$ is a uniformly random vertex of $\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}$.
    Since
    \begin{equation*}
       \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{\Gamma(a+1)}{\Gamma(a+k+1)} k a^{k-1} 
       = \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{ka^{k-1}}{(a+k)\cdots(a+1)}
       = \sum_{k\ge1} \left( \frac{a^{k-1}}{(a+k-1)\cdots(a+1)} - \frac{a^k}{(a+k)\cdots(a+1)} \right) = 1 \,,
    \end{equation*}
    we recognize that $\mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n) / \log n$ converges in distribution to a discrete random variable with probability mass function $k \mapsto \frac{ka^{k-1}}{(a+k)\cdots(a+1)}$ on $\mathbb{N}$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor: CLT descents q=a/n}
    Fix $a>0$.
    Then, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{{\mathrm{des}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}\right)}} - a\log n}{\sqrt{a \log n}}
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \mathcal{N}{\left(0,1\right)}
    \end{equation*}
    in distribution as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Fix $t>0$.
    Then, by Lemma~\ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime},
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ t^{{\mathrm{des}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n^{(a/n)}\right)}} / \sqrt{\log n}} \right]}
        = \frac{[x^n] A(x, t^{1/\sqrt{\log n}} a/n)}{[x^n] A(x,a/n)}
        \sim n^{a\left( t^{1/\sqrt{\log n}} - 1 \right)}
        \sim e^{a\log t \sqrt{\log n} + \frac{a}{2}\log^2 t} \,.
    \end{align*}
    Taking out $t^{a\sqrt{\log n}}$ on both sides, this yields the result by convergence of the moment generating function.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop: joint moments cv to CRT}
    Let $\ell\ge1$, and for each pair $(i,j)$ with $0\le i<j\le \ell$, let $r_{i,j} \in \mathbb{N}_0$.
    Then, with the same notation as in \Cref{th: CRT limit if q fixed},
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ \prod_{0\le i<j\le \ell}
        \left( \frac{q-1}{\sqrt q \log q} \,\frac{1}{\sqrt n}
        \,\mathrm{dist}_n{\left( W_{n,i} , W_{n,j} \right)} \right)^{r_{i,j}}
        \right]}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        \mathbb{E}{\left[ \prod_{0\le i<j\le \ell}
        \left(\big. \mathrm{dist}{\left( W_{i} , W_{j} \right)} \right)^{r_{i,j}}
        \right]} \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- head-2: body_md -->
$v_1=\rho(t)$.
Write $t_1, \dots, t_p$ for the subtrees rooted at the children of $v_1$, ordered in an arbitrary manner.
For each $k\in[p]$, let $\rho_k$ and $\mathrm{dist}_{t_k}$ denote the root and graph distance of $t_k$, respectively.
<!-- end head-2 -->
<!-- head-3: body_md -->
$v_1\ne \rho(t)$.
Write $t_0$ for the subtree of $t$ obtained by deleting $v_1$ and its descendants, and let $v_0 \in t_0$ be the parent of $v_1$.
As before, write $t_1, \dots, t_p$ for the subtrees rooted at the children of $v_1$, ordered arbitrarily.
<!-- end head-3 -->
<!-- head-4: body_md -->
Now, fix $(\ell,{\mathbf{r}})$ with $\ell\ge2$ and $\sum{\mathbf{r}} > 0$.
Assume that $\eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}$ holds for all the functions $f_{m,\bm{\alpha}}$ with $m<\ell$ and all the functions $f_{\ell,\bm{\alpha}}$ with $\bm{\alpha}\prec{\mathbf{r}}$.
<!-- end head-4 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotics first term Rellr tilde}
    For $\ell\ge2$ and $\sum{\mathbf{r}} > 0$, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} {\left[\big. Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) F_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \right]}\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}
        - Q_\ell(x,\bm{1}) f_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}(x)
        \;\sim\;
        q\, C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(1)}\,
        (1-x/x_0)^{-\ell - \frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}}
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$, where
    \begin{equation*}
        C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(1)} := \sum_{i=0}^\ell \sum_{\substack{0\prec \bm{\alpha}\preceq {\mathbf{r}} \\ \bm{\alpha} \text{ supported on } {\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}}} 
        \binom{{\mathbf{r}}}{\bm{\alpha}}
        C_{\ell, {\mathbf{r}}-\bm{\alpha}} C_{1,\sum\bm{\alpha}} 
    \end{equation*}
    is a positive constant.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotics second term Rellr tilde}
    If $\ell=2$, then as $x\to x_0$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} R_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right).
    \end{equation*}
    If $\ell\ge3$, then as $x\to x_0$,
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} R_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}
        \;\sim\;
        q\, C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(2)}\,
        (1-x/x_0)^{-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}},
    \end{equation*}
    where
    \begin{equation*}
        C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(2)} 
        = \sum_{\substack{ J_0 \sqcup J_1 = [0,\ell] \\ 0\in J_0,\, {\left|J_0\right|}<\ell,\, {\left|J_1\right|}<\ell }}
        \sum_{\substack{ \bm{0}\preceq \bm{\alpha}\preceq {\mathbf{r}} \\ \alpha_{i,j}=r_{i,j} \text{ for all } i,j\in J_0 \\  \alpha_{i,j}=0 \text{ for all } i,j\in J_1 }}
        \binom{{\mathbf{r}}}{\bm{\alpha}}
        C_{{\left|J_0\right|}, \bm\alpha^{J_0,\ell+1}}
        C_{{\left|J_1\right|}, ({\mathbf{r}}-\bm\alpha)^{0,J_1}} \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Here, for $(J_0,J_1)$ as above and $\bm{\alpha} \in \mathbb{N}_0^{{\rm B}_\ell}$, we let $\bm{\alpha}^{J_0,\ell+1}$ denote the element of $\mathbb{N}_0^{{\rm B}_{{\left|J_0\right|}}}$ defined by identifying $[0,{\left|J_0\right|}]$ with $J_0 \sqcup \{\ell+1\}$ (by the unique order-preserving bijection) and setting $\alpha^{J_0,\ell+1}_{i,j} := \alpha_{i,j}$ and $\alpha^{J_0,\ell+1}_{i,\ell+1} := \sum_{h\in J_1} \alpha_{i,h}$ for all $i,j\in J_0$.
    Moreover, we let $\bm{\alpha}^{0,J_1}$ denote the element of $\mathbb{N}_0^{{\rm B}_{{\left|J_1\right|}}}$ defined by identifying $[0,{\left|J_1\right|}]$ with $\{0\} \sqcup J_1$ and setting $\alpha^{0,J_1}_{i,j} = \alpha_{i,j}$ and $\alpha^{0,J_1}_{0,j} = \sum_{h\in J_0} \alpha_{h,j}$ for all $i,j\in J_1$.
    We set $C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(2)} := 0$ if $\ell=2$.
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Observe that
\begin{equation}\label{eq: Leibniz formula for product Qell Fell}
    \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left[\big. Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) F_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \right]
    - Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \big( \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} F_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \big)
    = \sum_{\bm{0} \prec \bm{\alpha} \preceq {\mathbf{r}}} 
    \left( \prod_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell} \binom{r_{i,j}}{\alpha_{i,j}} \right) 
                             
     \big(\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \big) \big( \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{{\mathbf{r}}-\bm{\alpha}} F_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \big)
\end{equation}
where the sum runs overs families of nonnegative integers $\bm{\alpha} = (\alpha_{i,j})_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell}$ such that $\bm{\alpha}\ne \bm{0}$ and $0\le \alpha_{i,j}\le r_{i,j}$ for all $\{i,j\}$, and ${\mathbf{r}}-\bm{\alpha} := (r_{i,j} - \alpha_{i,j})_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell}$.
Using \eqref{eq: def Q}, one could write a similar Leibniz formula for $\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}})$.
This allows us to directly identify the dominant term of $\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}$ as $x\to x_0$, depending on $\bm{\alpha}$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Assume that $\bm{\alpha}$ is not supported on any of the subsets ${\rm B}_\ell^{(i)} := \{ \{i,j\} : j\in[0,\ell], j\ne i\}$ for $i\in [0,\ell]$.
    Then, it is easily seen that $\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) = 0$.
    \item Assume that $\bm{\alpha}$ is supported on ${\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}$ for a unique $i\in[0,\ell]$.
    Then,
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}})
        = (\mathbbm{1}_{i=0} + q\mathbbm{1}_{i\ne0}) \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} \left[ \prod_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}} y_{i,j} \right]
        + q \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} \left[ F_1{\left(x, \prod_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}} y_{i,j}\right)} \prod_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}} y_{i,j}^{2
                    
        } \right] \,.
    \]
    Using \eqref{eq: claim singularity f1r}, the dominant term is obtained when differentiating solely the $F_1$ term in the product, and not the monomial in ${\mathbf{y}}$:
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}
                                                                                                                             
        \sim q f_{1,\sum\bm{\alpha}}(x)
        \sim q \frac{(\sum\bm{\alpha})!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^{\sum\bm{\alpha}} \left( 1 - {x}/{x_0} \right)^{-\frac12-\frac12\sum\bm{\alpha}}
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$.
    \item Assume that $\bm{\alpha}$ is supported on both ${\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}$ and ${\rm B}_\ell^{(j)}$ for some $\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell$, i.e., that the only non-zero term of $\bm{\alpha}$ is $\alpha_{i,j}$.
    Then, with the same analysis as the previous case,
    \begin{equation*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\bm{\alpha}} Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}
        \sim 2qf_{1,\alpha_{i,j}}(x)
        \sim 2q \frac{\alpha_{i,j}!}{\sqrt{2q}} \left( \sqrt{\frac{q}{2}} \frac{\log q}{q-1} \right)^{\alpha_{i,j}} \left( 1 - {x}/{x_0} \right)^{-\frac12-\frac12\alpha_{i,j}}
    \end{equation*}
    as $x\to x_0$.
\end{itemize}
Combining these cases and plugging them into \eqref{eq: Leibniz formula for product Qell Fell}, we get that
\begin{equation*}
    \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} {\left. \left[\big. Q_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) F_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} }
    - Q_\ell(x,\bm{1}) f_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}(x)
    \sim q\sum_{i=0}^\ell \sum_{\substack{0\prec \bm{\alpha}\preceq {\mathbf{r}} \\ \bm{\alpha} \text{ supported on } {\rm B}_\ell^{(i)}}} 
                                                                                      
    \binom{{\mathbf{r}}}{\bm{\alpha}}
    f_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}-\bm{\alpha}}(x) f_{1,\sum\bm{\alpha}}(x)
\end{equation*}
as $x\to x_0$.
Finally, using the induction hypothesis, this concludes the proof.
The fact that the constant $C_{\ell,{\mathbf{r}}}^{(1)}$ is positive is straightforward.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Recall that $R_{\ell}$ is defined by \eqref{eq: equadiff on Fell with everything} after taking out the terms containing the function $F_\ell$:
\begin{align}
    R_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}})
    = &\sum_{s=0}^\ell \sum_{\substack{\mathcal{J}\in \mathcal{P}_{1,s}^\mathrm{red} \\ {\left|J_k\right|}<\ell \text{ for all } k\in[1,s]}} \frac{1}{s!} 
    M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}})
    \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}\left(\big. x , \varphi_{J_k}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) 
    \label{eq: def Rell first line}\\
    &+ q \sum_{s=0}^\ell \sum_{\substack{\mathcal{J}\in \mathcal{P}_{2,s}^\mathrm{red} \\ {\left|J_k\right|}<\ell \text{ for all } k\in[0,s]}} \frac{1}{s!} 
    M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}})
    F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left(\big. x , \psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right)
    \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}\left(\big. x , \varphi_{J_k}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \,. 
    \label{eq: def Rell second line}
\end{align}
Let us analyze the asymptotics, as $x\to x_0$, of each term appearing in $\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} R_\ell(x,{\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}}$, starting with the first line \eqref{eq: def Rell first line} of the formula.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Let $s=0$ and $\mathcal{J}$ be the unique partition in $\mathcal{P}_{1,0}^\mathrm{red}$.
    The corresponding term is $\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) \vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} = \mathcal{O}(1)$.
    \item Let $s=1$ and $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,1}^\mathrm{red}$ be such that ${\left|J_1\right|}<\ell$.
    Then $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-{\left|J_1\right|}} \right)$ by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}.
    Furthermore, each differentiation step changes this exponent by $0$ or $-\frac12$, so that
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-(\ell-1)-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
    \item Let $s\ge2$ and $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{1,s}^\mathrm{red}$.
    Then $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac{s}{2}-\sum_{1\le k\le s}{\left|J_k\right|}} \right)$ by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}.
    Furthermore, each differentiation step changes this exponent by $0$ or $-\frac12$, so that
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_k}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac{s}{2}-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
\end{itemize}
Each case above yields a $o\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right)$.
Now we turn to the second line \eqref{eq: def Rell second line}, which is handled similarly.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Let $s=0$ and $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,0}^\mathrm{red}$ be such that ${\left|J_0\right|}<\ell$.
    Then $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-{\left|J_0\right|}} \right)$ by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}.
    Furthermore, each differentiation step changes this exponent by $0$ or $-\frac12$, so that
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-(\ell-1)-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
    \item Let $s=1$ and $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,1}^\mathrm{red}$ be such that ${\left|J_0\right|}<\ell$ and ${\left|J_1\right|}<\ell$.
    Then, by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}, $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{1-{\left|J_0\right|}-{\left|J_1\right|}} \right)$.
    Furthermore, each differentiation step changes this exponent by $0$ or $-\frac12$, so that
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{1-(\ell+1)-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
    Let us analyze this case with more care.
    Specifically, we wish to identify which $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,1}^\mathrm{red}$ yield an optimal exponent, that is, $-\ell$, before differentiating.
    First, assume $\ell=2$.
    Then, ${\left|J_0\right|}+{\left|J_1\right|} = \ell+1 = 3$ would imply either ${\left|J_0\right|}=2$ or ${\left|J_1\right|}=2$, which is forbidden in \eqref{eq: def Rell second line}.
    So, if $\ell=2$, we can improve to
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{1-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
    Next, assume $\ell\ge3$.
    Then, there exist partitions $(J_0,J_1)$ of $[0,\ell]$ with $0\in J_0$, ${\left|J_0\right|}<\ell$ and ${\left|J_1\right|}<\ell$.
    For such a partition, we have $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \Theta\left( (1-x/x_0)^{-\ell} \right)$ by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}.
    Furthermore, to reach the optimal exponent $-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}$ after differentiating, one needs to differentiate one of the two $F$ terms at each differentiation step.
    Recall that the family $\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}})$ is defined in terms of $(y_{i,j})_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(J_0)}}$ and the family $\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}})$ is defined in terms of $(y_{i,j})_{\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(J_1)}}$.
    Recall also that $J_0 \sqcup J_1 = [0,\ell]$.
    Therefore, when differentiating $r_{i,j}$ times with respect to $y_{i,j}$, there are the following cases:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If $\{i,j\}\in {\rm B}_\ell^{(J_0)} \cap {\rm B}_\ell^{(J_1)}$, that is, if $i\in J_0$ and $j\in J_1$ (or vice versa), then we get to choose which $F$ term to differentiate.
        Differentiating $F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) )$, resp.\ $F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) )$, with respect to $y_{i,j}$ yields
        \[
            \left( \prod_{h\in J_1 \setminus \{j\}} y_{i,h} \right) \left[ \partial_{i,\ell+1} F_{{\left|J_0\right|}} \right]( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) ) \,,
            \quad\text{resp.}\quad
            \left( \prod_{h\in J_0 \setminus \{i\}} y_{h,j} \right) \left[ \partial_{0,j} F_{{\left|J_1\right|}} \right]( x,\psi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) ) \,.
        \]
        Here, the first derivative is with respect to the argument of $F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}$ indexed by $\{i,\ell+1\}$, where we identify $[0,{\left|J_0\right|}]$ with $J_0 \sqcup \{\ell+1\}$.
        The second derivative is with respect to the argument of $F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}$ indexed by $\{0,j\}$, where we identify $[0,{\left|J_1\right|}]$ with $\{0\} \sqcup J_1$.
        \item Otherwise, $\{i,j\} \in {\rm B}_\ell^{(J_0)}$ if and only if both $i$ and $j$ are in $J_0$.
        In that case, we have to differentiate the $F_{\left|J_0\right|}$ term.
        Differentiating $F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) )$ with respect to $y_{i,j}$ yields
        \[
            \left[ \partial_{i,j} F_{{\left|J_0\right|}} \right]( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) ) \,,
        \]
        where the first derivative is with respect to the argument of $F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}$ indexed by $\{i,j\}$, where we identify $[0,{\left|J_0\right|}]$ with $J_0 \sqcup \{\ell+1\}$.
        \item Likewise, if both $i$ and $j$ are in $J_1$, we have to differentiate the $F_{\left|J_1\right|}$ term.
        The function $F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) )$ has derivative with respect to $y_{i,j}$ given by
        \[
            \left[ \partial_{i,j} F_{{\left|J_1\right|}} \right]( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) ) \,.
        \]
        Here, the derivative is with respect to the argument of $F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}$ indexed by $\{i,j\}$, where we identify $[0,{\left|J_1\right|}]$ with $\{0\} \sqcup J_1$.
    \end{itemize}
    In the end we get, with the notation of the lemma,
    \begin{multline*}
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} {\left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) F_{{\left|J_1\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_1}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} } 
        \\\sim
        \sum_{\substack{ \bm{0}\preceq \bm{\alpha}\preceq {\mathbf{r}} \\ \alpha_{i,j}=r_{i,j} \text{ for all } i,j\in J_0 \\ \alpha_{i,j}=0 \text{ for all } i,j\in J_1}}
                                                                                    
        \binom{{\mathbf{r}}}{\bm{\alpha}}
        f_{{\left|J_0\right|}, \bm\alpha^{J_0,\ell+1}}(x)
        f_{{\left|J_1\right|}, ({\mathbf{r}}-\bm\alpha)^{0,J_1}}(x) 
    \end{multline*}
    \item Let $s\ge2$ and $\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{P}_{2,s}^\mathrm{red}$.
    Then $M_\mathcal{J}(\bm{1}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}(x,\bm{1}) = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac{s+1}{2}-\sum_{1\le k\le s}{\left|J_k\right|}} \right)$ by the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}.
    Furthermore, each differentiation step changes this exponent by $0$ or $-\frac12$, so that
    \[
        \partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\mathbf{r}} \left. \left[ M_\mathcal{J}({\mathbf{y}}) F_{{\left|J_0\right|}}\left( x,\psi_{J_0}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \prod_{1\le k\le s} F_{{\left|J_k\right|}}\left( x,\varphi_{J_k}({\mathbf{y}}) \right) \right] \right\vert_{{\mathbf{y}}=\bm{1}} 
        = \mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac{s+1}{2}-(\ell+1)-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right) \,.
    \]
\end{itemize}
If $\ell=2$, then each case above yields a $\mathcal{O}\left( (1-x/x_0)^{\frac12-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right)$.
If $\ell\ge3$, then the penultimate case yields a $\Theta\left( (1-x/x_0)^{-\ell-\frac12\sum{\mathbf{r}}} \right)$, while the other cases are negligible.
Using the induction hypothesis \eqref{eq: claim singularity fellr}, the conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- head-5: body_md -->
Now, fix $(\ell,{\mathbf{r}})$ with $\ell\ge2$ and $\sum{\mathbf{r}} > 0$.
Assume that $\eqref{eq: claim dependency Cellr on q}$ holds for all the indices $(m,\bm\alpha)$ with either $m<\ell$, or $m=\ell$ and $\bm{\alpha}\prec{\mathbf{r}}$.
Let us write $\mathcal{C}_{\ell, {\mathbf{r}}}(q)$ for the family of constants $C_{m,\bm\alpha}(q)$ with either $m<\ell$, or $m=\ell$ and $\bm{\alpha}\prec{\mathbf{r}}$.
<!-- end head-5 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop: joint convergence of tilde j tilde c tilde n towards PoissonDirichlet}
Let $q_n=a/n$ and $\mathcal{T}_n = \mathcal{T}_n^{(q_n)}$.
The $\ell$-tuples $\mathbf{j}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n)$, $\mathbf{c}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n)$, $\mathbf{n}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n)$ asymptotically jointly approximate a single Poisson--Dirichlet random variable.
More precisely, let $\Delta$ denote the infinite-dimensional simplex endowed with the product topology, and let $d_{\Delta^3}$ be any metric for the weak topology on $\mathcal{M}_1(\Delta^3)$. 
Embed 
                                                                                                      
$\{(x_1,\ldots,x_\ell) \in \mathbb{R}_+^\ell : \sum_{i=1}^\ell x_i = 1\}$
naturally in $\Delta$ by completing an element with infinitely many $0$'s. Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{k\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} 
        d_{\Delta^3}{\left( \mathcal{L}{\left( \frac1k\mathbf{j}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n) , \frac{1}{a\log k} \mathbf{c}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n) , \frac1n \mathbf{n}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)} \;,\; \mathcal{L}{\left( X^{(a)} , X^{(a)} , X^{(a)} \right)} \right)} 
        = 0
    \end{equation*}
    where $X^{(a)} \sim \mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)}$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic probability of V1}
For any fixed $k\ge1$,
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k)\right)} \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, \frac{k}{a+k} \,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:combinatorial formulas}
    Fix a non-plane labeled tree $\widetilde t \in \mathbb{T}_\ell$ with size $\ell$ and out-degrees $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}} = (c_\upsilon)_{1\le \upsilon\le \ell}$.
    Moreover, fix $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}} = (j_\upsilon)_{1\le \upsilon\le \ell}$, a list of positive integers summing to $k$, and $\widetilde{\mathbf{n}} = (n_\upsilon)_{1\le \upsilon\le \ell}$, a list of positive integers summing to $n$, such that $n_\upsilon\ge j_\upsilon$ for all $\upsilon\in[\ell]$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \begin{multline*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^\mathrm{red} = \widetilde t , \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    \\= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell!Z_{n,q}} q^{\ell-1}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    (n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon) ^{c_\upsilon}
    \cdot [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) \,.
    \end{multline*}

\item  Conditionally given $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\mathcal{T}_n)$, the reduced tree $\widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^{k,\mathrm{red}}$ is a uniform labeled tree with out-degrees $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\mathcal{T}_n)$.

\item \begin{multline*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{c}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    \\= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell Z_{n,q}} q^{\ell-1}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    \frac{(n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon)^{c_\upsilon}}{c_\upsilon!}
    \cdot [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) \,.
    \end{multline*}

\item \begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    = \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon}] x^{j_\upsilon} e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) \,.
    \end{align*}

\item \begin{align}\label{item: proba reduced tree has small root and tildej}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\right)}
    = \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]}
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q) \,.
    \end{align}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Fix $\ell\ge1$ and sum equation~\eqref{item: proba reduced tree has small root and tildej} of Lemma~\ref{lem:combinatorial formulas} over all $\ell$-tuples $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$ of positive integers summing to $k$:
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \ell(\mathcal{T}_n)=\ell\right)}
    = \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)}
    \sum_{\substack{j_1, \dots, j_\ell \ge1\\ j_1+\dots+j_\ell =k}} \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]}
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q).
\end{align*}
Thus, using the fact that $[x^0] A(x,q) = 0$,
\begin{align}\label{eq: reduced tree distribution ell}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \ell(\mathcal{T}_n)=\ell\right)}
    = \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)}
    [x^{k}] A(x,q)^\ell \,.
\end{align}
Finally, by summing \eqref{eq: reduced tree distribution ell} over $\ell\ge1$, we obtain
\begin{align}\label{eq: reduced tree distribution V1}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k)\right)}
    = \frac{k!(n-k)!}{Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{-1}
    [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)}
    [x^{k}] e^{q(n-k)A(x,q)} \,.
\end{align}
    Let us take $n\to\infty$ in \eqref{eq: reduced tree distribution V1} (recall that $k\ge1$ is fixed, and that $q=a/n$).
    By Lemma \ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient partition function in transition regime}, we know that 
    \begin{equation*}
        Z_{n,q} = n! [x^n] A(x,q) \sim \frac{n! n^{a-1}}{e^a \Gamma(a+1)} \,,
    \end{equation*}
    by Lemma \ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient exponentials in transition regime} that 
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)} \sim \frac{k n^{a-1+k}}{e^a\Gamma(a+k+1)} \,,
    \end{equation*}
    and by the standard enumeration of recursive trees that 
    \begin{equation*}
        [x^{k}] e^{q(n-k)A(x,q)} 
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, [x^k] e^{a A(x,0)}
        = [x^k] (1-x)^{-a}
        = \frac{\Gamma(a+k)}{\Gamma(a)k!} \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Plugging all of these into \eqref{eq: reduced tree distribution V1}, we find that $\mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k)\right)} \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \frac{k}{a+k}$ as $n \rightarrow \infty$, as desired.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We can compute the probability $\mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^\mathrm{red} = \widetilde t , \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}$ by describing the permutation/tree pairs $(\sigma,T)$ satisfying these conditions.
To construct such a tree, one needs to choose $t_u$'s and $F_u$'s with sizes given by the $j_u$'s, then $\tau_i$'s and $\Phi_i$'s with positive sizes $\nu_i$ such that $\sum_{i\in F_u} \nu_i = n_u$, and consistent $p_v$'s.
Note also that once the partition $\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}$ has been chosen, the permutation $\sigma$ is uniquely determined.
We get, by decomposing the tree $\mathcal{T}_n$ according to the structure of its reduced tree,
\begin{align*}
    &\ell! \, Z_{n,q} \cdot \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^\mathrm{red} = \widetilde t , \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    \\&= q^{\ell-1} \sum_{(t_\upsilon)_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \in \prod\mathbb{T}_{j_\upsilon}} 
    \! \left( \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_\upsilon\right)}} \right) \!\!
    \sum_{\substack{\widetilde{\mathbf{F}} \vdash [k]\\ {\left|F_\upsilon\right|}=j_\upsilon \\ \text{for all }\upsilon}}
    \sum_{\substack{(\nu_i)_{i\in[k]} \in \mathbb{N}^k\\ \sum_{i\in F_\upsilon}\nu_i = n_\upsilon \\ \\ \text{for all }\upsilon}}
    \sum_{\substack{(\tau_i)_{i\in[k]} \in \prod\mathbb{T}_{\nu_i}\\ \text{root labeled }1}} 
    \binom{n-k}{\nu_1-1, \dots, \nu_k-1}
    \prod_{1\le i\le k} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(\tau_i\right)}}
    \\&\hspace{1.1cm} 
    \cdot \prod_{\substack{v \in \tilde t\setminus\rho\\\text{parent }=:\, u(v)}} \left( n_{u(v)} - j_{u(v)} \right)
    \\&= q^{\ell-1} (n-k)!
    \left( \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \sum_{t_\upsilon \in \mathbb{T}_{j_\upsilon}} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_\upsilon\right)}} \right) \!\!\!
    \sum_{\substack{\widetilde{\mathbf{F}} \vdash [k]\\ {\left|F_\upsilon\right|}=j_\upsilon \\ \text{for all }\upsilon}}
    \sum_{\substack{(\tau_i)_{i\in[k]} \in \mathbb{T}^k\\ \text{root labeled }1}}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{i \in F_\upsilon}{\left|\tau_i\right|} = n_\upsilon}
    \left( n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon \right)^{c_\upsilon}
    \prod_{i\in F_\upsilon} \frac{q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(\tau_i\right)}}}{({\left|\tau_i\right|}-1)!}
    \right) \,.
\end{align*}
Now, observe that the term inside the sum $\sum_{\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}}$  does not depend on the partition $\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}$.
Therefore, we can fix an arbitrary partition $\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}$ and write
\begin{align*}
    &\ell! \, Z_{n,q} \cdot \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^\mathrm{red} = \widetilde t , \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    \\&= q^{\ell-1} (n-k)!
    \left( \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \sum_{t_\upsilon \in \mathbb{T}_{j_\upsilon}} q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_\upsilon\right)}} \right) \!
    \binom{k}{\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}}
    \sum_{\substack{(\tau_i)_{i\in[k]} \in \mathbb{T}^k\\ \text{root labeled }1}}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{i \in F_\upsilon}{\left|\tau_i\right|} = n_\upsilon}
    \left( n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon \right)^{c_\upsilon}
    \prod_{i\in F_\upsilon} \frac{q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(\tau_i\right)}}}{({\left|\tau_i\right|}-1)!}
    \right) 
    \\&= q^{\ell-1} k!(n-k)!
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    (n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon)^{c_\upsilon}
    \sum_{t_\upsilon \in \mathbb{T}_{j_\upsilon}} \frac{q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(t_\upsilon\right)}}}{j_\upsilon!}
    \sum_{\substack{(\tau_i)_{i\in F_\upsilon} \in \mathbb{T}^{F_\upsilon}\\ \text{root labeled }1}}
    \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{i \in F_\upsilon}{\left|\tau_i\right|} = n_\upsilon}
    \prod_{i\in F_\upsilon} \frac{q^{\mathrm{des}{\left(\tau_i\right)}}}{({\left|\tau_i\right|}-1)!}
    \right) 
    \\&= q^{\ell-1} k!(n-k)!
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    (n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon)^{c_\upsilon}
    \cdot [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] \left( \partial_x A^{(1)}(x,q) \right)^{j_\upsilon}
    \right)
\end{align*}
where $A^{(1)}(x,q)$  is the generating function for descent-biased trees with root labeled $1$.
By \Cref{lem:trees with smallest root}, we deduce Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas}~(i). 

Hence, the probability that $\widetilde \mathcal{T}_n^{k,\mathrm{red}} = \widetilde t$ only depends on $\widetilde t$ through its sequence of out-degrees $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$. This immediately implies Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas}~(ii).

Then, recall that the number of labeled trees $\widetilde t \in \mathbb{T}_\ell$ with out-degrees $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$ is $\frac{(\ell-1)!}{\prod\limits_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} c_\upsilon!}$. Therefore, using Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas}~(i), we get Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas}~(iii).

Now, sum over all $\ell$-tuples $\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}$ of non-negative integers summing to $\ell-1$:
\begin{align*}
    &\mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
    \\&= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell Z_{n,q}} q^{\ell-1} \!\!\sum_{\substack{(c_\upsilon)_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \in \mathbb{N}_0^\ell\\\sum c_\upsilon = \ell-1}}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    \frac{(n_\upsilon - j_\upsilon)^{c_\upsilon}}{c_\upsilon!}
    \cdot [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) 
    \\&= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell Z_{n,q}} q^{\ell-1}
    [x^{\ell-1}] 
    \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \left( 
    \sum_{c\ge0} \frac{(n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon)^c x^c}{c!} 
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) 
    \\&= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell Z_{n,q}} q^{\ell-1}
    [x^{\ell-1}] e^{x(n-k)}
    \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \left( 
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q)
    \cdot [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \right) \,,
\end{align*}
which implies Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas} (iv).

Finally, sum over all $\ell$-tuples $\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}$ of non-negative integers summing to $n$ (the condition $n_\upsilon\ge j_\upsilon$ appears naturally in the formula):
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\right)}
    &= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    \left( \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]}
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q) \right)
    \sum_{n_1+ \dots+ n_\ell = n} \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} [x^{n_\upsilon}] x^{j_\upsilon} e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)}
    \\&= \frac{k!(n-k)!}{\ell! Z_{n,q}} \left(q(n-k)\right)^{\ell-1}
    \left( \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]}
    [x^{j_\upsilon}] A(x,q) \right)
    \cdot 
    [x^n] \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} x^{j_\upsilon} e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)} \,,
\end{align*}
which is Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas} (v).
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic distribution tilde j Ewens}
    Conditionally on $V_\rho(k)$, the sequence $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n) := (j_{(1)}\ge \dots\ge j_{(\ell)})$ 
                                                            
    converges in distribution as $n\to\infty$ to the non-increasing ordering of 
                     
    cycle lengths in a Ewens random permutation with size $k$ and parameter $a$.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Take $n\to\infty$ in Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas}~(v), using $A(x,q) \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, A(x,0) = -\log(1-x)$ along with the same asymptotics as before.
    This yields, for any $\ell$-tuple $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$ of positive integers summing to $k$,
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}\right)}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        k!
        \frac{ k\Gamma(a+1)}{\Gamma(a+k+1)}
        \frac{a^{\ell-1}}{\ell!}
        \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \frac{1}{j_\upsilon} \,.
    \end{align*}
    Thus, by \Cref{lem: asymptotic probability of V1},
    \begin{align}
    \label{eq:ewens1}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}  \,\right|\, V_\rho(k) \right)}
        \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,
        k!
        \frac{\Gamma(a)}{\Gamma(a+k)}
        \frac{a^{\ell}}{\ell!}
        \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \frac{1}{j_\upsilon} \,.
    \end{align}
    Now let $\pi\in\mathfrak{S}_k$ be Ewens-distributed with parameter $a$, and let $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\pi_k)$ be its sequence of cycle lengths after a uniformly random ordering.
    Then,
    \begin{align}
    \label{eq:ewens2}
        \mathbf{P}{\left( \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\pi_k) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}} \right)}
        = \frac{a^\ell}{Z_{k,a}}
        \cdot \frac{1}{\ell!} 
        \cdot\binom{k}{\mathbf{j}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} (j_\upsilon-1)!
        = \frac{k!}{Z_{k,a}}
        \frac{a^{\ell}}{\ell!}
        \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \frac{1}{j_\upsilon} \,,
    \end{align}
    where $Z_{k,a} = \frac{\Gamma(a+k)}{\Gamma(a)}$ is the partition function for the Ewens model.
    Comparing Equations \eqref{eq:ewens1} and \eqref{eq:ewens2} concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor: asymptotic concentration ell}
    We have
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{k\to\infty} \limsup_{n\to\infty} {\left| \frac{\ell^k(\mathcal{T}_n)}{a\log k} - 1 \right|} = 0
    \end{equation*}
    in probability.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor: asymptotic distribution tilde j PoissonDirichlet}
    Let $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ be the non-increasing reordering of $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)$.
    Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{k\to\infty} \lim_{n\to\infty} \mathcal{L}{\left( \frac1k \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)} = \mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)}
    \end{equation*}
    weakly on the infinite-dimensional simplex.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem: proportionality tilde j tilde n}
    The $\ell$-tuples $\frac1n \mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ and $\frac1k\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ are close in the following sense:
    for any $\delta>0$,
    \begin{align*}
    \limsup_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(d_\infty{\left( \frac1k \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n), \frac1n \mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)}
    \ge \delta\right)} 
    \,\underset{k\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 0 \,.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem: proportionality tilde j tilde c}
    The $\ell$-tuples $\frac{1}{\ell-1} \mathbf{c}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ and $\frac1k\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ are close in the following sense:
    for any $\delta>0$,
    \begin{align*}
    \limsup_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(d_\infty{\left( \frac1k \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n), \frac{1}{\ell^k(\mathcal{T}_n)-1} \mathbf{c}'(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)}
    \ge \delta\right)} 
    \,\underset{k\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 0 \,.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
\Cref{prop: joint convergence of tilde j tilde c tilde n towards PoissonDirichlet} follows directly from \Cref{cor: asymptotic distribution tilde j PoissonDirichlet,cor: asymptotic concentration ell} together with \Cref{lem: proportionality tilde j tilde n,lem: proportionality tilde j tilde c}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic density of tilde n conditional tilde j}
    Fix $\ell\ge2$ and a sequence of positive integers $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}=(j_1, \dots, j_\ell)$ summing to $k$.
    Conditionally on the events $V_\rho(k)$ and $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$, the $\ell$-tuple $\frac1n \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)$ converges in distribution, as $n\to\infty$, to the density 
    \begin{equation*}
        \alpha \mapsto \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k} \prod_{\upsilon\in [\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon \cdot \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a + j_\upsilon+1)}
    \end{equation*}
    with respect to the Lebesgue measure on the ($\ell\!-\!1$)--dimensional simplex 
    \[
        \Delta_{\ell} := \left\{ (\alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_{\ell-1}) \;:\; \alpha_1,\dots,\alpha_{\ell-1} \ge 0 ,\, \alpha_1+\dots+\alpha_{\ell-1} \le 1 \right\} \,,
    \]
    seen as a compact subset of $\mathbb{R}^{\ell-1}$, where we write $\alpha_\ell := 1 - (\alpha_1+\dots+\alpha_\ell)$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor: asymptotic density of n1 conditional tilde j}
    Fix $\ell\ge2$ and a non-increasing sequence of positive integers $\mathbf{j}'=(j_1 \ge \dots \ge j_\ell)$ summing to $k$.
    Conditionally on the events $V_\rho(k)$ and $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n) = \mathbf{j}'$, for each $i\in[\ell]$, the random variable~$\frac1n n_i'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ converges in distribution to the density
    \begin{equation*}
        f_{k,j_i}: \alpha \mapsto \frac{j_i(k-j_i)}{k} \Gamma(a+k+1) \frac{\alpha^{\alpha a+j_i-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha a + j_i +1)} \frac{(1-\alpha)^{(1-\alpha)a+k-j_i-1}}{\Gamma((1-\alpha)a+k-j_i+1)}
    \end{equation*}
    on $[0,1]$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas} (iv) and (v), we have, for any sequence $\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}$ of positive integers summing to $n$,
    \begin{align}\label{eq: reduced tree distribution tilde n conditional tilde j}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \Big.\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{n}} \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}} \right)} 
        = \frac{1}{ [x^{n-k}] e^{k A(x,q)} } \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} [x^{n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon}] e^{j_\upsilon A(x,q)} \,.
    \end{align}
    To work out the asymptotics related to this distribution, we rely on Lemma \ref{lem: asymptotic coefficient exponentials in transition regime} implying that for all $\alpha\in[0,1]$, all $n \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\alpha n \in \mathbb{N}$ and all $j\ge1$,
    \begin{align}\label{eq: estimate coefficient xalphan of expA transition regime uniform in alpha}
        [x^{\alpha n}] e^{jA(x,q)} 
        = [x^{\alpha n}] e^{jA(x, \frac{\alpha a}{\alpha n})}
