        = \frac{j (\alpha n)^{\alpha a+j-1} e^{-\alpha a}}{\Gamma(\alpha a+j+1)} \left(1+o(1)\right),
    \end{align}
    where the $o$ is uniform in $\alpha$. Now, let $h$ be a continuous map on $\Delta_\ell$.
    Taking $n\to\infty$ in \eqref{eq: reduced tree distribution tilde n conditional tilde j} with the asymptotics \eqref{eq: estimate coefficient xalphan of expA transition regime uniform in alpha}, we get
    \begin{align*}
        &\mathbb{E}{\left[\left.  h{\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)\right)}  \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}} \right]} 
        \\&= \sum_{\substack{j_\upsilon\le n_\upsilon\le n \text{ for all }\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\\ n_{\ell}:= n- \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} n_\upsilon}}
        h{\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \Big. \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{n}} \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}} \right)} 
        \\&= \sum_{\substack{j_\upsilon\le n_\upsilon\le n \text{ for all }\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\\ n_{\ell}:= n- \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} n_\upsilon}}
        \mathbbm{1}_{n_\ell\ge j_\ell}
        h{\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
        \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k n^{a+k-1} e^{-a}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon (n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon)^{(n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon) a/n +j_\upsilon -1} e^{-(n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon) a/n}}{\Gamma((n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon) a/n+j_\upsilon+1)} \left(1+o(1)\right)
        \\&= \sum_{\substack{j_\upsilon\le n_\upsilon\le n \text{ for all }\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\\ n_{\ell}:= n- \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} n_\upsilon}}
        \mathbbm{1}_{n_\ell\ge j_\ell}
        h{\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
        \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k n^{a+k-1} e^{-a}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon n_\upsilon^{n_\upsilon a/n +j_\upsilon -1} e^{-n_\upsilon a/n}}{\Gamma(n_\upsilon a/n+ j_\upsilon +1)} \left(1+o(1)\right)
        \\&= \sum_{\substack{j_\upsilon\le n_\upsilon\le n \text{ for all }\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\\ n_{\ell}:= n- \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} n_\upsilon}}
        \mathbbm{1}_{n_\ell\ge j_\ell}
        h{\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}\right)}
        \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k n^{\ell-1}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon (n_\upsilon/n)^{n_\upsilon a/n +j_\upsilon -1}}{\Gamma(n_\upsilon a/n+ j_\upsilon +1)} \left(1+o(1)\right)\,,
    \end{align*}
    where the $o$ is uniform in the $n_\upsilon$'s.
    One recognizes a (truncated) $(\ell-1)$--dimensional Riemann sum for the continuous map 
    \begin{equation*}
        (\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_{\ell-1}) \mapsto 
        h{\left(\alpha\right)}
        \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon +1)}
    \end{equation*}
    on the ($\ell\!-\!1$)--dimensional simplex 
    (where we write $\alpha_\ell := 1 - \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} \alpha_\upsilon$), yielding
    \begin{align}
    \label{eq:strange computation}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[\left.  h\left(\frac1n\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)\right)  \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}} \right]} 
        \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k} 
        \int_{0}^{1} \mathrm{d}\alpha_1 \ldots \int_{0}^{1} \mathrm{d}\alpha_{\ell-1} \mathbbm{1}_{\alpha_\ell\ge0}
        h(\alpha) \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon -1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon +1)}
    \end{align}
    as $n\to\infty$.
    This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
In particular, \Cref{eq:strange computation} provides a computation of the nontrivial integral
    \begin{equation*}
        \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k} \int_{0}^{1} \mathrm{d}\alpha_1 \ldots \int_{0}^{1} \mathrm{d}\alpha_{\ell-1} \mathbbm{1}_{\alpha_\ell\ge0}
        \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon -1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon +1)}
        = 1 \,,
    \end{equation*}
    where $\alpha_\ell := 1 - \sum_{\upsilon<\ell} \alpha_\upsilon$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We need to compute the marginal distribution of $\alpha :=\alpha_{i}$, where $(\alpha_1, \dots, \alpha_\ell)$ is the weak limit of $\frac1n\mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ conditionally given $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$.
Since \Cref{lem: asymptotic density of tilde n conditional tilde j} holds for $\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}$ when conditioning on $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$, it also holds for $\mathbf{n}'$ when conditioning on $\mathbf{j}'$, after reordering the vectors to make $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$ non-increasing (with ties broken arbitrarily).
Therefore, we get that $\alpha$ has density
\begin{align*}
    \alpha \mapsto \frac{\Gamma(a+k+1)}{k} \frac{j \alpha^{\alpha a +j-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha a+j+1)} 
                                                                                    
    \int_{[0,1]^{\ell-2}} \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \alpha_\upsilon \le 1-\alpha}
    \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon+1)} 
    \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \mathrm{d}\alpha_{\upsilon}
\end{align*}
on $[0,1]$, where we write $j:=j_i(\mathcal{T}_n)$ and $\alpha_\ell := 1-\alpha - \sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \alpha_\upsilon$.
In order to compute that integral, define $b := (1-\alpha) a$ and, for any $v \in [\ell]\setminus\{i\}$, $\beta_\upsilon = \frac{\alpha_\upsilon}{1-\alpha}$. 
Then, we have $\beta_\upsilon b=\alpha_\upsilon a$, and we can rewrite
\begin{align*}
    \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon+1)} &= \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon (\beta_\upsilon(1-\alpha))^{\beta_\upsilon b +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon+1)}\\
    &= (1-\alpha)^{\sum (\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon-1)} \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \beta_\upsilon^{\beta_\upsilon b +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon+1)} \\
    &= (1-\alpha)^{(1-\alpha) a-(\ell-1)+k-j} \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \beta_\upsilon^{\beta_\upsilon b +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon+1)},
\end{align*}
where we have used that $\sum\limits_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \beta_\upsilon = 1$, $b=(1-\alpha)a$, and $\sum\limits_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} j_\upsilon = k-j$.
We also know from Lemma \ref{lem: asymptotic density of tilde n conditional tilde j} that 
\begin{equation*}
    (\beta_\upsilon, \upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}) \mapsto \frac{\Gamma(b+k-j+1)}{k-j} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \beta_\upsilon^{\beta_\upsilon b +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon +1)}
\end{equation*}
is a probability density on $\Delta_{\ell-1}$, where we write $\beta_\ell := 1 - \sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \beta_\upsilon$. 
Hence, we have
\begin{align*}
    &                                                 
    \int_{[0,1]^{\ell-2}} \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \alpha_\upsilon \le 1-\alpha}
    \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \alpha_\upsilon^{\alpha_\upsilon a +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\alpha_\upsilon a+j_\upsilon +1)} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}}\mathrm{d}\alpha_\upsilon 
    \\&= (1-\alpha)^{\ell-2} 
                                                            
    \int_{[0,1]^{\ell-2}} \mathbbm{1}_{\sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}} \beta_\upsilon \le 1}
    (1-\alpha)^{(1-\alpha) a-(\ell-1)+k-j} \prod_{\upsilon \in [\ell] \backslash \{i\}} \frac{j_\upsilon \beta_\upsilon^{\beta_\upsilon b +j_\upsilon-1}}{\Gamma(\beta_\upsilon b+j_\upsilon +1)} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell-1]\setminus\{i\}}\mathrm{d}\beta_\upsilon \\
    &= (1-\alpha)^{(1-\alpha) a-1+k-j} \frac{k-j}{\Gamma((1-\alpha)a+k-j+1)} \,.
\end{align*}
Altogether, we find that $\alpha$ has the desired density on $[0,1]$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We will show that, conditionally on $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ being ``typical'', the vector $\frac1n \mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ is concentrated around $\frac{1}{k} \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$.
Let $i\in\mathbb{N}$ be fixed.
Thanks to \Cref{cor: asymptotic concentration ell}, we can assume with high probability that $i \le \ell$.
Write $j := j_i(\mathcal{T}_n)$ and let $f_{k,j}(\alpha)$ be the asymptotic density of $\frac1n n_i(\mathcal{T}_n)$ found in \Cref{cor: asymptotic density of n1 conditional tilde j}.
Fix $\varepsilon \in (0,1/2)$, and let $\beta = \frac{j}{k}$. 
We have the following approximation, uniformly in $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$ and in $\alpha \in (0,1)$, as $k \rightarrow \infty$:
\begin{align*}
    f_{k,j}(\alpha) &=\Gamma(a+k+1) \frac{j(k-j)}{2k\pi \sqrt{(\alpha a +j)((1-\alpha)a+k-j)}} \frac{\alpha^{\alpha a-1+j}}{\left(\frac{\alpha a +j}{e}\right)^{\alpha a +j}} \frac{(1-\alpha)^{(1-\alpha) a-1+k-j}}{\left(\frac{(1-\alpha) a +k-j}{e}\right)^{(1-\alpha) a +k-j}} (1+o(1))\\
    &=\Gamma(a+k+1)\frac{\sqrt{\beta(1-\beta)}}{2\pi} e^{a+k} \frac{1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)} \left( \frac{\alpha}{\alpha a+j} \right)^{\alpha a + j} \left( \frac{1-\alpha}{(1-\alpha)a+k-j} \right)^{(1-\alpha)a+k-j} (1+o(1)) \,.
\end{align*}
We now expand
\begin{align*}
\log\left(\left( \frac{\alpha}{\alpha a+j} \right)^{\alpha a +j}\right) 
= (\alpha a + j) \big( \log(\alpha/j)+\mathcal{O}(1/k) \big)
= j \log(\alpha/j) + \mathcal{O}(\log k) \,,
\end{align*}
again uniformly for $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$ and $\alpha \in (0,1)$. 
Hence,
\begin{align*}
    \log &\left( \frac{1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)} \left( \frac{\alpha}{\alpha a+j} \right)^{\alpha a + j} \left( \frac{1-\alpha}{(1-\alpha)a+k-j} \right)^{(1-\alpha)a+k-j} \right)
    \\ &\qquad \qquad= j \log(\alpha/j) + (k-j)\log((1-\alpha)/(k-j)) - \log(\alpha(1-\alpha)) + \mathcal{O}(\log k)
    \\&\qquad \qquad= \left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac1k\right)\right) \left[\Big. j \log(\alpha/j) + (k-j) \log((1-\alpha)/(k-j)) \right]
    \\&\qquad \qquad= \left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac1k\right)\right) k \left[\Big. \beta \log(\alpha/\beta)+ (1-\beta) \log((1-\alpha)/(1-\beta)) - \log k \right] \,,
\end{align*}
                                                                                                                  
again uniformly for $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$ and $\alpha \in (0,1)$. 
The function $g: \alpha\mapsto  \beta \log(\alpha/\beta)+ (1-\beta) \log((1-\alpha)/(1-\beta))$ is strictly concave and reaches its only maximum at $\alpha_*=\beta$, where $g(\alpha_*)=0$. 
Furthermore, $g''(\alpha)<-1$ for any $\alpha \in (0,1)$ and any $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$. 
Thus, Taylor's formula provides the following:
\begin{itemize}
    \item for all $\alpha$ such that $|\alpha-\alpha_*|<k^{-1/2}$, there exists $\gamma$ between $\alpha$ and $\alpha_*$ such that
    \begin{align*}
    g(\alpha)
    =g(\alpha_*)+\frac{g''(\gamma)}{2}(\alpha-\alpha_*)^2
    \ge \frac{1}{2k} g''(\alpha_*)(1+o(1))
    \end{align*}
    as $k \rightarrow \infty$, uniformly in $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$.
    \item for all $\alpha$ such that $|\alpha-\alpha_*|>k^{-1/4}$, there exists $\gamma$ between $\alpha$ and $\alpha_*$ such that
    \begin{align*}
    g(\alpha)
    =g(\alpha_*)+\frac{g''(\gamma)}{2}(\alpha-\alpha_*)^2
    \le -\frac{1}{2\sqrt{k}} \,.
    \end{align*}
\end{itemize}
Thus, we get
\begin{multline*}
    \left(\Gamma(a+k+1) e^{a+k} \frac{\sqrt{\beta(1-\beta)}}{2\pi}\right)^{-1}\int_{|\alpha-\alpha_*|<k^{-1/2}} f_{k,j}(\alpha) \mathrm d\alpha 
    \\= (1+o(1)) \int_{|\alpha-\alpha_*|<k^{-1/2}} e^{-k \log k}e^{kg(\alpha)} e^{\mathcal{O}(g(\alpha)-\log k)} \mathrm d\alpha
    \ge 2k^{-1/2}e^{-k \log k} e^{-\mathcal{O}(\log k)} \,,
\end{multline*}
while
\begin{align*}
    \left(\Gamma(a+k+1) e^{a+k} \frac{\sqrt{\beta(1-\beta)}}{2\pi}\right)^{-1} \int_{|\alpha-\alpha_*| 
    > k^{-1/4}} f_{k,j}(\alpha) \mathrm d\alpha 
    \le e^{- C\sqrt{k}}e^{-k \log k}e^{-\mathcal{O}(\log k)},
\end{align*}
for some $C>0$ independent of $\beta \in [\varepsilon, 1-\varepsilon]$. 
Hence, we get that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: cv of alpha}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  {\left|\alpha_i-\frac{j_i(\mathcal{T}_n)}{k}\right|} \le k^{-1/4} \quad \,\right|\, \quad j_i(\mathcal{T}_n) \in [\varepsilon k, (1-\varepsilon)k]  \right)} \,\underset{k\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,,
\end{equation}
where $i\in\mathbb{N}$ is fixed and $\alpha_i$ is distributed under the $f_{k,j_i}$ density.
We can now conclude the proof. 
Fix $\eta>0$. 
Using the Poisson--Dirichlet asymptotics of $\frac1k \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$ from \Cref{cor: asymptotic distribution tilde j PoissonDirichlet}, we get the following. 
There exist $\varepsilon>0$ and $p\ge1$ such that, for all $1\le i\le p$ and all large enough $n$, $\mathbf{P}{\left(j_i'(\mathcal{T}_n) \in [\varepsilon k, (1-\varepsilon)k]\right)} \geq 1-\eta$, and in addition
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\sum_{i=1}^p j_i' \ge (1-\eta)k\right)} 
    \ge 1-\eta \,.
\end{align*}
Using \eqref{eq: cv of alpha} for each of the $j_i'$'s, $1 \leq i \leq p$, we get the result.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
From Lemma \ref{lem:combinatorial formulas} (iii) and (iv), we deduce that
    \begin{equation}\label{eq: reduced tree distribution tilde c conditional tilde j tilde n}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big.\widetilde{\mathbf{c}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{c}} \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \widetilde{\mathbf{j}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}, \widetilde{\mathbf{n}}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\widetilde{\mathbf{n}} \right)}
        = \frac{(\ell-1)!}{(n-k)^{\ell-1}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{(n_\upsilon-j_\upsilon)^{c_\upsilon}}{c_\upsilon!} \,.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore, the same formula holds when reordering all vectors to make $\widetilde{\mathbf{j}}$ non-increasing.
    For fixed $i$ ($1\le i\le \ell$), let us derive the conditional distribution of $c_i'(\mathcal{T}_n)$. We have
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. c_i'(\mathcal{T}_n)=c_i' \,\right|\, V_\rho(k), \mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n)=\mathbf{j}', \mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n)=\mathbf{n}' \right)} \nonumber
        &= \sum_{\substack{c_\upsilon' \ge 0, \upsilon\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}\\\sum_{\upsilon\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}} c_\upsilon'=\ell-1-c_i'}} \frac{(\ell-1)!}{(n-k)^{\ell-1}} \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]} \frac{(n_\upsilon'-j_\upsilon')^{c_\upsilon'}}{c_\upsilon'!} \nonumber
        \\&= \frac{(\ell-1)! (n_i'-j_i')^{c_i'}}{(n-k)^{\ell-1} c_i'!} [x^{\ell-1-c_i'}] \prod_{\upsilon\in[\ell]\setminus\{i\}} e^{x(n_\upsilon'-j_\upsilon')} \nonumber
        \\&= \frac{(\ell-1)! (n_i'-j_i')^{c_i'}}{(n-k)^{\ell-1} c_i'!} \frac{(n-n_i'-k+j_i')^{\ell-1-c_i'}}{(\ell-1-c_i')!} \,.
    \end{align*}
    That is, the conditional law of $c_i'(\mathcal{T}_n)$, under the event $V_\rho(k)$ and given $\mathbf{j}'(\mathcal{T}_n), \mathbf{n}'(\mathcal{T}_n)$, is $\mathrm{Bin}{\left(\ell-1,\frac{n_i'-j_i'}{n-k}\right)}$.   
    It remains to show the desired concentration result for this distribution.

    Fix an arbitrary $p\in(0,1)$.
    For a given $\varepsilon>0$, let $E_\varepsilon$ be the event that $\frac1k j_i'(\mathcal{T}_n) \in [\varepsilon,1-\epsilon]$.
    We know from \Cref{cor: asymptotic distribution tilde j PoissonDirichlet} that there exists a small enough $\varepsilon = \varepsilon(p)$, a $k_0$ and for each $k$ an $m_0(k)$, such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all }k\ge k_0 \text{ and for all }n\ge m_0(k),\quad
        \mathbf{P}{\left(E_\varepsilon\right)} \ge 1-p \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Then, fix $0< \delta\le \frac13\varepsilon$ and use \Cref{lem: proportionality tilde j tilde n}.
    There exists $k_1$, and for each $k$ there exists $m_1(k)$, such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all }k\ge k_1 \text{ and for all }n\ge m_1(k),\quad
        \mathbf{P}{\left( {\left| \frac1k j_i'(\mathcal{T}_n) - \frac1n n_i'(\mathcal{T}_n) \right|} \le \delta \right)} \ge 1-p \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Let $E_\delta'$ be the above event.
    Under $E_\delta'$, we have ${\left| \frac{n_i'-j_i'}{n-k} - \frac1k j_i' \right|} \le \delta + \frac{2k}{n-k}$.
    For each $k$, let $m_2(k)$ be such that $\frac{2k}{n-k} \le \delta$ for all $n\ge m_2(k)$.
    Under the events $E_\varepsilon$ and $E_\delta'$ and for any $n\ge m_2(k)$, we have ${\left| \frac{n_i'-j_i'}{n-k} - \frac1k j_i' \right|} \le 2\delta$, and further $\frac{n_i'-j_i'}{n-k} \in [\frac13\varepsilon, 1-\frac13\varepsilon]$.
    
    For each $\ell\ge2$ and $q\in[0,1]$, write $S_{\ell,q}$ for a $\mathrm{Bin}{\left(\ell-1,q\right)}$ random variable.
    Then, there exists $\ell_0$ such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all }q\in[\tfrac13\varepsilon, 1-\tfrac13\varepsilon] \text{ and for all }\ell\ge\ell_0,\quad
        \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\frac{S_{\ell,q}}{\ell-1}-q\right|} \le \delta\right)} \ge 1-p \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, again by \Cref{cor: asymptotic distribution tilde j PoissonDirichlet}, there exists $k_3$, and for each $k$ there exists $m_3(k)$, such that:
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all }k\ge k_3 \text{ and for all }n\ge m_3(k),\quad
        \mathbf{P}{\left( \ell(\mathcal{T}_n) \ge \ell_0 \right)} \ge 1-p \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Combining all of the above, we find that
    \begin{equation*}
        \text{for all } k\ge \max(k_0,k_1,k_3),\text{ and for all } n\ge \max(m_0,m_1,m_2,m_3),\quad
        \mathbf{P}{\left( {\left| \frac{c_i'(\mathcal{T}_n)}{\ell(\mathcal{T}_n)-1} - \frac1k j_i'(\mathcal{T}_n) \right|} \le 3\delta \right)} \ge 1-4p \,,
    \end{equation*}
    for any fixed $i\in\mathbb{N}$.
    The conclusion follows in the same way as in the proof of \Cref{lem: proportionality tilde j tilde n}.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:finite foata fuchs}
Fix $m \in \mathbb{N}$ and consider a sequence ${\textbf{x}} := (x_1,\ldots,x_{m-1}) \in [m]^{m-1}$. We build a tree $T_\textbf{x} \in \mathbb{T}_m$ from $\textbf{x}$ as follows. 
Let $D(\textbf{x})$ be the set of elements of $[m]$ which do not appear in $\textbf{x}$.
Let $(u_1, \ldots, u_m)$ be the vertices of $T_\textbf{x}$, where $u_i$ has label $i$. Then,
\begin{itemize}
    \item the root of $T_\textbf{x}$ is the vertex $u_{x_1}$;
    \item if $x_2 \neq x_1$, then $u_{x_2}$ is a child of $u_{x_1}$;
    \item if $x_2=x_1$, then $u_{d_1}$ is a child of $u_{x_1}$, where $d_1=\min D(\textbf{x})$.
    \item Iteratively, we have, for $3\le i \leq m-1$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item if $x_i \notin \{x_1,\ldots,x_{i-1}\}$, then $u_{x_i}$ is a child of $u_{x_{i-1}}$;
        \item if $x_i \in \{x_1,\ldots,x_{i-1}\}$, then $u_d$ is a child of $u_{x_{i-1}}$, where $d$ is the smallest element of $D(\textbf{x})$ not used yet.
    \end{itemize}
    \item Finally, $u_d$ is a child of $u_{x_{m-1}}$, where $d$ is the only remaining element in $D(\textbf{x})$.
\end{itemize}
One can check that the tree $T_\textbf{x}$ constructed that way is indeed an element of $\mathbb{T}_m$, and that the out-degree of a vertex $u_i$ is exactly the number of occurrences of $i$ in $\textbf{x}$.
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:foata fuchs}
Fix $m \geq 1$, and define the set 
\[
    C_m:= \Big\{ (c_1,\ldots,c_m) \in \mathbb{N}_0^m : \sum_{i=1}^m c_i=m-1 \Big\} \,.
\] 
For any $\mathbf{c} := (c_1,\ldots,c_m) \in C_m$, let $\mathbb{T}_{m,\mathbf{c}}$ be the subset of $\mathbb{T}_m$ consisting of all trees whose sequence of out-degrees is $\mathbf{c}$ (that is, such that the vertex labeled $i$ has $c_i$ children, for all $i \in [m]$). 
Then,
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(i)] the Foata--Fuchs construction is a bijection from $[m]^{m-1}$ to $\mathbb{T}_m$;
    \item[(ii)] for any $\mathbf{c} \in C_m$, the Foata--Fuchs construction is a bijection from $[m]^{m-1}_{\mathbf{c}}$ to $\mathbb{T}_{m,\mathbf{c}}$.
\end{itemize}
Here, $[m]^{m-1}_{\mathbf{c}}$ denotes the set of $(m\!-\!1)$-tuples of elements of $\{1,\ldots,m\}$ with $c_i$ occurrences of $i$, for all $i \in [m]$.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
In \Cref{def:finite foata fuchs}, we explain how to construct a tree given its degree sequence.
    This is the main use of the Foata--Fuchs bijection that we will need.
    For completeness, let us briefly describe the inverse map as well:
    consider a tree $T\in\mathbb{T}_m$ with vertices $(u_1, \dots, u_m)$, where $u_i$ has label $i$.
    Let $\mathbf{c}$ be the degree sequence of $T$; we wish to construct $\textbf{x}\in[m]^{m-1}_\mathbf{c}$ such that $T = T_\textbf{x}$.
    
    First, let $u_d$ be the leaf of $T$ with lowest label and let $P_1 := (u_{x_1}, \dots, u_{x_j}, u_d)$ denote the shortest path from the root of $T$ to this leaf.
    We set the first $j$ elements of $\textbf{x}$ to $(x_1, \dots, x_j)$.
    Then, let $u_{d'}$ be the leaf of $T$ with the second lowest label.
    Let $P_2 := (u_{y_1}, \dots, u_{y_{j'}}, u_{d'})$ denote the shortest path from $P_1$ to $u_{d'}$.
    In particular, $y_1 \in \{x_1, \dots, x_j\}$.
    The next elements of $\textbf{x}$ are then defined as $(y_1, \dots, y_{j'})$ (notice that, then, $y_1$ appears for the second time in $\textbf{x}$).
    
    This procedure goes on iteratively: at each step, we follow the shortest path from the previously visited vertices to the leaf with the lowest label not yet visited.
    In this procedure, we follow each edge $e$ precisely once, and this corresponds to an occurrence (in $\textbf{x}$) of the label of the endpoint of $e$ that is closer to the root. 
    Hence, one can check that $\textbf{x}\in[m]^{m-1}_\mathbf{c}$. See, e.g., \cite{Addario-Berry_Blanc-Renaudie_Donderwinkel_Maazoun_Martin_2023} for details.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{def:countable foata}
Consider a sequence $\textbf{z} := (z_1,z_2,\ldots) \in \mathbb{N}^\mathbb{N}$ of positive integers. 
We construct a real tree $(T_\textbf{z},\rho_\textbf{z},d_\textbf{z})$ from it as follows.
\begin{itemize}
    \item We first build a discrete tree $T^{\operatorname{disc}}(\textbf{z})$ with countably many vertices denoted by $\{v_1,v_2, \ldots\}$:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item the root $\rho_\textbf{z}$ of the tree is $v_{z_1}$;
        \item for all $j \geq 2$ such that $z_j \notin \{z_1,\ldots,z_{j-1}\}$, $v_{z_j}$ is a child of $v_{z_{j-1}}$, and we draw an edge between them.    
        \end{itemize}
    \item Then, $(T_\textbf{z},\rho_\textbf{z},d_\textbf{z})$ is the real tree obtained from the discrete tree $T^{\operatorname{disc}}(\textbf{z})$ by regarding each edge as an interval of length $1$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def: size-biased dendron}
Fix $a>0$ and let $(X_1,X_2,\ldots)$ be distributed as a Poisson--Dirichlet variable $\mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)}$. 
Then, define a sequence $\textbf{Z} := (Z_j)_{j \geq 1}$ of i.i.d.\ random variables such that $\mathbf{P}{\left(Z_1=i\right)}=X_i$, for all $i \in \mathbb{N}$. 
We construct the random dendron $\mathcal{D}_a:=(T_a,\rho_a,d_a,\nu_a)$ as follows. 
\begin{itemize}
    \item The base tree $(T_a,\rho_a,d_a)$ is the tree $(T_{\textbf{Z}},\rho_\textbf{Z},d_\textbf{Z})$ built from $\textbf{Z}$ by the countably infinite Foata--Fuchs construction of Definition \ref{def:countable foata};
    \item the mass measure $\nu_a$ on $T_a \times [0,\infty)$ is defined as
    \begin{align*}
        \nu_a = \sum_{i \geq 1} X_i \delta_{(v_i,1)} \,,
    \end{align*}
    where $(v_i)_{i\ge1}$ are the vertices of the discrete tree $T^{\operatorname{disc}}(\textbf{Z})$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:convergence of distances critical window}
For any $p \geq 1$, the (random) matrix 
\begin{align*}
    \left( \frac{1}{\log n} d{\left( w_r^{(n)}, w_s^{(n)} \right)} \right)_{1 \leq r,s \leq p}
\end{align*}
converges in distribution to the random matrix $(d_a(w_{r}, w_{s}))_{1 \leq r,s \leq p}$, where $w_{1}, \ldots, w_{p}$ are i.i.d.\ points on $\mathcal{D}_a$ drawn according to the measure $\nu_a$.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
With the exact same ingredients, one can prove that, for all $p \geq 1$, the random matrix 
\begin{align*}
    \left( \frac{1}{\log n} d{\left( w_r^{(n)}, w_s^{(n)} \right)} \right)_{0 \leq r,s \leq p} \,,
\end{align*}
with the convention that $w_0^{(n)}$ is the root of $\mathcal{T}_n$,
converges in distribution to the random matrix $(d_a(w_{r}, w_{s}))_{0 \leq r,s \leq p}$, where $w_{1}, \ldots, w_{p}$ are i.i.d. points on $\mathcal{D}_a$ drawn according to the measure $\nu_a$ and $w_{0} := (\rho_a,0)$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:not many descents on the ancestral line}
    Conditionally given the tree $\mathcal{T}_n$, let $u_n$ be a uniformly random vertex in $\mathcal{T}_n$. 
    Then,
    \begin{align*}
      \liminf_{n\to\infty} \mathbf{P}{\left( D_{u_n}(\mathcal{T}_n) \subseteq \{1, \ldots, K\} \right)} \,\underset{K\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:structure_of_permutations}
Let ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}$ be the number of descents of a permutation $\sigma$.
Fix $a > 0$ and let $q_n := a/n$.
Fix also $0<c<C$. 
For $c\log n\le h\le C\log n$, let $\sigma^{h}$ be a descent-biased permutation with size $h$ and bias $a/n$.
Following \cite{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}, we write $S^{(a/n)}_{h}$ for the law of $\sigma^h$.
Then, we have the following.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item For all $1\le r\le h$, let $\sigma^{h,r}$ be distributed as $S^{(a/n)}_{h}$, conditionally on its last element $\sigma_h=r$.
    The number of descents of $\sigma^{h,r}$ is uniformly stochastically bounded: 
    \begin{align*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma^{h,r}\right)}} \le Q\right)} \,\underset{Q\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,.
    \end{align*}
    \item For all $d\ge 1$, let $\sigma^{h,r,d}$ be distributed as $S^{(a/n)}_{h}$, conditionally on $\sigma_h=r$ and ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}=d$. 
    For a permutation~$\sigma$, let $D(\sigma)=\{\sigma_i : 2\le i\le h, \sigma_{i-1} > \sigma_i\}$ be the set of endpoints of its descents.
    Then, for all fixed $d\ge1$,
    \begin{align*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(D(\sigma^{h,r,d}) \subseteq \{1,\ldots,K\} \right)} \,\underset{K\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,.
    \end{align*}
    \item For $d=1$, the first element of $\sigma^{h,r,d}$ can take any finite value less than $r$ with probability uniformly bounded from below.
    That is, for all fixed $K\ge1$, we have
    \begin{align*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h} \inf_{1\le j\le \min(K,r-1)}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\sigma_1^{r,h,d} = j\right)} > 0 \,.
    \end{align*}
    \item For $d\ge2$, the first element of $\sigma^{h,r,d}$ can take any finite value, except $r$, with probability uniformly bounded from below.
    That is, for all fixed $d\ge2$ and $K\ge1$, we have
    \begin{align*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h} \inf_{j\in [1,K] \setminus \{r\}}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\sigma_1^{r,h,d} = j\right)} > 0 \,.
    \end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. First, by Proposition \ref{prop: asymptotic coefficient typical depth in transition regime}, there exist $0<c<C$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\[
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n)\in [c \log n, C \log n]\right)} \geq 1-\varepsilon \,. 
\]
Write $h := \mathrm{dist}(\rho,u_n)$ for ease of notation.
Let $\rho =: v_0, \ldots, v_{h} := u_n$ be the ancestors of $u_n$, $v_j$ having height $j$, and let $L(v_0), \ldots, L(v_{h})$ be their labels.
Write $L_i$ for the $i$-th smallest of these labels, and let $r$ be such that $L_r = L(u_n)$.
We call $r = \operatorname{rk}(u_n)$ the rank of $u_n$; by definition, $u_n$ has precisely $r-1$ ancestors with labels lower that its own.
Now, perform the following shuffling operation on the tree $\mathcal{T}_n$:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Let $\sigma \in \mathfrak{S}_{h+1}$ be distributed as $S_{h+1}^{(a/n)}$, conditionally on $\{ \sigma_{h+1} = r \}$. 
    \item Let $(v'_j)_{0 \leq j \leq h-1}$ be the vertices $(v_j)_{0 \leq j \leq h-1}$ reordered so that $v'_{j-1}$ has label $L_{\sigma_j}$.
    \item For $0\le j\le h-1$, write $C_j$ for the connected component of $\mathcal{T}_n \backslash \bigcup_{i=1}^{h}\{(v_{i-1}, v_{i})\}$ containing $v_j$.
    Then reconnect the $C_j$'s according to this new order dictated by $\sigma$.
    In particular, $v'_0$ is now the root of the tree that we obtain.
\end{itemize}
Let $\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_n$ be the tree obtained this way. 
Then, clearly, $(\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_n, u_n) \overset{(d)}{=} (\mathcal{T}_n,u_n)$.
See \Cref{fig: shuffling} for an illustration.

\begin{figure}
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=.45\linewidth]{reshuffling.pdf}
    \caption{Example of an ancestral reshuffling.
    Left: 
    the vertex $u_n$ has height $h=3$ and label $4$.
    The ranks along its ancestral line are $3,4,1,2$.
    In particular, $r=2$.
    Right: 
    the resulting tree after shuffling the ancestral line with $\sigma = 1\,3\,4\,2$.}
    \label{fig: shuffling}
\end{figure}

Observe that $\big|D_{u_n}\big(\tilde\mathcal{T}_n\big)\big|= {\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}$, and furthermore that $D_{u_n}\big(\tilde\mathcal{T}_n\big)$ can be identified with $D(\sigma)$ via the ranks of the labels.
By Lemma \ref{lem:structure_of_permutations}~(i), there exists $Q>0$ such that
\begin{align*}
\liminf_{n\to\infty} 
\inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  {\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}} \le Q \; \,\right|\, \, h(u_n) = h, \operatorname{rk}(u_n)=r \right)} \ge 1-\varepsilon \,.
\end{align*}
From now on, we restrict ourselves to the case ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}\le Q$. 
                                                                                                
From \Cref{lem:structure_of_permutations}~(ii), we get that for all $1\le d\le Q$,
\begin{align*}
\liminf_{n\to\infty} 
\inf_{c\log n \le h \le C\log n}\inf_{1\le r\le h} 
\mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  D_{u_n}\big(\tilde\mathcal{T}_n\big) \subseteq \{L_1,\ldots,L_K\} \,  \,\right|\,  \, {\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}=d, h(u_n)=h, \operatorname{rk}(u_n)=r \right)} \,\underset{K\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,.
\end{align*}
Hence, we can choose $K$ so large that, with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, the labels of all descent endpoints are among the smallest $K$ labels in the ancestral line of $u_n$.
It remains to deduce that these labels are stochastically bounded.

We know by \Cref{lem: asymptotic probability of V1} that the root label of the tree is asymptotically stochastically bounded.
In addition, we have by \Cref{lem:structure_of_permutations}~(iv) that for all $2\le d\le Q$,
\begin{equation*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} 
    \inf_{c\log n\le h\le C\log n} \inf_{1\le r\le h} \inf_{\substack{1\le j\le K \\ j\ne r}} 
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  L(v_0') = L_j \, \,\right|\, \, {\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}=d, h(u_n)=h, \operatorname{rk}(u_n)=r \right)} > 0 \,.
\end{equation*}
The latter extends the stochastic boundedness of the root label $L_{\sigma_1}$ to a stochastic boundedness for the smallest $K$ labels $L_1, \ldots, L_K$, conditionally on ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}\geq 2$. This concludes the proof when ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}\geq 2$.

When ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}=1$, we just need to use the fact that $D_{u_n}\big(\mathcal{T}_n\big)$ contains at most one label which is among the $r-1$ smallest ones.
We can therefore conclude as before, using \Cref{lem:structure_of_permutations}~(iii).
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Let us start by proving (i). 
Let $r\in[h]$.
Write $Z_{h,r,a/n}$ for the partition function of descent-biased permutations conditioned on $\sigma_h=r$, that is,
\begin{equation*}
    Z_{h,r,a/n} := \sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_h} \mathbbm{1}_{\sigma_h=r} (a/n)^{{\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma\right)}}} \,.
\end{equation*}
Then, let us bound the probability of ${\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma^{h,r}\right)}}\ge Q$.
To this aim, we can use \cite[Corollary~16~(ii)]{Thevenin_Wagner_2023} which bounds the number of permutations with a given number of descents and a given first value.
Thanks to the bijection $\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_h \mapsto \left( n+1-\sigma_{n+1-i} \right)_{1\le i\le n}$, we know that the number of permutations with $d$ descents and ending with $r$ equals the number of permutations with $d$ descents and starting with $n-r+1$.
Therefore,
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma^{h,r}\right)}}\ge Q\right)}
    \le Z_{h,r,a/n}^{-1} \sum_{d\ge Q} d^{r-1} (d+1)^{h-r} (a/n)^d
    \le Z_{h,r,a/n}^{-1} \sum_{d\ge Q} (d+1)^{h} (a/n)^d \,.
\end{align*}
In order to bound the partition function, define $\tau$ as the cycle $(r,r+1, \dots, h)$ (if $r=h$, then $\tau=\operatorname{id}_h$).
In particular $\tau_{h}=r$, and $\tau$ has at most one descent so that $\mathbf{P}{\left(\sigma^{h,r} = \tau\right)} \ge Z_{h,r,a/n}^{-1} \cdot (a/n)$. 
This further entails that $Z_{h,r,a/n}^{-1} \le (a/n)^{-1}$, and thus
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma^{h,r}\right)}}\ge Q\right)}
    \le \sum_{d\ge Q} (d+1)^{h} (a/n)^{d-1} \,.
\end{align*}
Under the assumption $h\le C\log n$, we get
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\mathrm{des}{\left(\sigma^{h,r}\right)}}\ge Q\right)} 
    \le a^{-1} \sum_{Q\le d\le C\log n} n^{C\log(d+1) + (C\log a)^+ - d + 1}
\end{align*}
where $(C\log a)^+ = \max(0, C\log a)$.
If $Q$ is large enough, this goes to $0$ as $n\to\infty$, proving (i).

In order to prove (ii), (iii) and (iv), we rely on a construction of $\sigma^{h,r,d}$ that is similar to the one presented in \cite[Lemma~15]{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}. 
Fix $d\ge 1$. 
Let $A_1, \ldots, A_{r-1}$ be i.i.d.~random variables, uniform on $\{1,\ldots,d+1\}$, and $A_{r+1}, \ldots, A_h$ be i.i.d.~random variables, uniform on $\{1,\ldots,d\}$. 
For $1\le i\le d+1$, let $L_i := \{j: A_j = i\}$. 
Let finally $\tau^{h,r,d}$ be the permutation obtained by concatenating $L_1,\ldots,L_{d+1},\{r\}$, where the elements in each $L_i$ are placed in increasing order. 
Then, uniformly for $h \in [c\log n, C\log n]$ and $r \in [h]$, $\mathbf{P}{\left(\tau^{h,r,d} \text{ has } d \text{ descents}\right)} \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1$; 
furthermore, under this event, $\tau^{h,r,d}$ is distributed as $\sigma^{h,r,d}$. 
We omit the proof, which is the exact same as for \cite[Lemma~15]{Thevenin_Wagner_2023}.
Now observe that, for any $K \geq 1$ and any $1 \leq i \leq d$, we have
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\min L_i \geq K\right)} 
= \prod\limits_{\substack{j=1 \\ j\ne r}}^{K-1} \mathbf{P}{\left(A_j \neq i\right)}
\le \left( \frac{d}{d+1} \right)^{K-2} \,,
\end{equation*}
which goes to $0$ as $K \rightarrow \infty$. 
Furthermore, for $i=d+1$, we have 
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\min L_{d+1} \cup \{r\} \geq K\right)} 
= \mathbbm{1}_{r \geq K} \prod\limits_{j=1}^{K-1} \mathbf{P}{\left(A_j \ne d+1\right)} 
\le \left( \frac{d}{d+1} \right)^{K-1} \,,
\end{align*}
which also goes to $0$ as $K \rightarrow \infty$. 
This proves (ii).
For (iii) and (iv), it remains to find an adequate lower bound on the probability that $\tau^{h,r,d}_1 = j$.
If $j<r$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left( \tau^{h,r,d}_1 = j \right)}
    = \mathbf{P}{\left(A_j=1\right)} \prod_{1\le i< j} \mathbf{P}{\left(A_i \ne 1\right)}
    = \left( \frac{d}{d+1} \right)^{j-1} \cdot \frac{1}{d+1} \,,
\end{equation*}
whereas for $j>r$ we have
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left( \tau^{h,r,d}_1 = j \right)}
    = \mathbf{P}{\left(A_j=1\right)} \prod_{\substack{1\le i< j\\i\ne r}} \mathbf{P}{\left(A_i \ne 1\right)}
    = \left( \frac{d}{d+1} \right)^{r-1} \cdot \left( \frac{d-1}{d} \right)^{j-r-1} \cdot \frac{1}{d} \,,
\end{equation*}
which is nonzero whenever $d \geq 2$.
The conclusion follows.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:no small descent}
Assume that $k_n ! = o(q_n^{-1})$.
Then, with high probability, there is no descent edge between two vertices with labels $\le k_n$ in $\mathcal{T}_n$.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Let us call a tree $t\in\mathbb{T}_n$ \enquote{bad} if it contains a descent edge between two labels $\le k_n$, and \enquote{good} otherwise.
    Recall that $Z_{n,q} = \sum_{t\in\mathbb{T}_n} q_n^{{\mathrm{des}{\left(t\right)}}}$ denotes the partition function of our model.
    Let $\check{Z}_{n,q}$ denote the restriction of this sum to bad trees $t$.
    Observe that any bad tree $t\in\mathbb{T}_n$ can be mapped to a good tree $t'\in\mathbb{T}_n$ through a reordering of the small labels.
    This map strictly decreases the number of descents, and any given good tree $t'$ has at most $k_n!$ preimages under this map.
    Therefore,
    \[
        \check Z_{n,q} \le k_n! \cdot q_n \cdot Z_{n,q} \,,
    \]
    and the claim follows readily.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:lemmerelou4bis}
For all fixed $L \geq 1$ and all $\varepsilon>0$, there exists $\eta>0$ such that
\begin{align*}
    \liminf_{n\to\infty} \mathbf{P}{\left( (n)_i^{k_n} \geq \eta n \text{ for all } i\in[L]\;\right)} \geq 1-\varepsilon.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
This follows from \Cref{eq:kn} and the fact that, for all $i \geq 1$, the $i$-th element of a $\mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)}$ random variable has a density on $\mathbb{R}_+^*$.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:lemmerelou5bis}
For any $L \geq 1$, we have
\begin{align*}
    \sup\limits_{1 \leq i \leq L} \frac{N^{(k_n)}\big( (\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n} \big)}{(n)_i^{k_n}} \overset{\mathbf{P}}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 0
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. By \Cref{lem:lemmerelou4bis},  there exists $\eta>0$ such that
    \begin{align*}
        \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathbf{P}{\left( (n)_i^{k_n} < \eta n \text{ for some } i \in [L]\right)} < \varepsilon.
    \end{align*}
    Now fix $\eta'>0$, and let $u_n$ denote a uniformly random vertex of $\mathcal{T}_n$.
    By \Cref{prop:not many descents on the ancestral line}, there exists $k_0 = k_0(\eta,\eta',\varepsilon) \ge 1$ such that, for all $k \geq k_0$, $\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}{\left( \max D_{u_n}(\mathcal{T}_n) \ge k+1 \right)} \le \eta \eta' \varepsilon$. 
    Since $k_n\to\infty$, we deduce by monotonicity that
    \begin{equation*}
        \limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{P}{\left( \max D_{u_n}(\mathcal{T}_n) \ge k_n+1 \right)} \le \eta \eta' \varepsilon \,.
    \end{equation*}
    This implies by Markov's inequality that
    \begin{align*}
        \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(|E_{n}| \geq \eta \eta' n\right)} \le \varepsilon,
    \end{align*}
    where $E_{n} := \{ u\in \mathcal{T}_n, \max D_u \geq k_n+1\}$.
    On the other hand, under the event $|E_{n}| \leq \eta \eta' n$, for all $i \in [L]$ such that $(n)_i^{k_n} \geq \eta n$, we have $N^{(k_n)}((\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n}) \leq \eta \eta' n \leq \eta' (n)_i^{k_n}$. 
    Therefore,
    \begin{equation*}
        \limsup_{n\to\infty} \mathbf{P}{\left( N^{(k_n)}\big( (\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n} \big) > \eta' (n)_i^{k_n} \text{ for some } i\in[L]\right)}
        \le 2\varepsilon \,.
    \end{equation*}
    Since this holds for any $\varepsilon>0$ and $\eta'>0$, we get the result.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def: M structure of discrete tree}
Fix $k$ and $M$ with $k \geq M \geq 1$, and fix a tree $T\in\mathbb{T}_n$ with $n$ vertices labeled from $1$ to $n$. 
Assume that the root label of $T$ is $\le k$.
The $M$-structure $\operatorname{St}_M^{(k)}(T)$ is the element of $\mathbb{T}_M^{\geq}$ that is constructed as follows.
\begin{itemize}
    \item Relabel the vertices of $T^{k,\mathrm{red}}$ as $u_{(1)}, \ldots, u_{(\ell)}$, by non-increasing order of the sizes $[n]_{u_{(1)}}^k \ge \ldots \ge [n]_{u_{(\ell)}}^k$ of the associated components (with arbitrary choice in case of equality);
    \item give the label $i$ to $u_{(i)}$, for $1 \leq i \leq \ell$;
\end{itemize}
Then, define $\operatorname{St}_M^{(k)}(T)$ as the subtree of $T^{k,\mathrm{red}}$ spanned by $u_{(1)}, \ldots, u_{(\min(M,\ell))}$, forgetting the decorations and keeping only the labels defined above. 
By construction, $\operatorname{St}_M^{(k)}(T)$ is an element of $\mathbb{T}_M^{\geq}$.
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
By \Cref{eq:concentration ell}, we get that $\ell^{k_n}(\mathcal{T}_n)/\log k_n \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, a$ in probability as $n \to \infty$. 
   In particular, we have 
    \[
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\min(M,\ell^{k_n}(\mathcal{T}_n)) = M\right)} \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 1 \,.
    \]
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
Recall the definition of the dendron $\mathcal{D}_a:=(T_a,\rho_a,d_a,\nu_a)$ with measure $\nu_a := \sum_{i \geq 1} X_i \delta_{(v_i,1)}$, with the notation of Definition~\ref{def: size-biased dendron}. 
The $M$-structure $\operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)$ is the subtree of $T_a$ spanned by $v_1, \ldots, v_M$. 
Giving the label $i$ to each vertex $v_i$ makes it an element of $\mathbb{T}_M^{\geq}$.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:proba of a structure}
Fix $M \geq 1$. 
Then the following statements hold:
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(i)] On the Skorokhod space where \Cref{eq:kn,eq:concentration ell} hold a.s., we have, for any given $\mathfrak{T}\in \mathbb{T}_M^{\geq}$,
    \[
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, \mathbf{c}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)} 
        \overset{a.s.}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 
        \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \Big. \operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)} \,.
    \]
    \item[(ii)] Let $\max \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n)$ be the largest label of a vertex in the $M$-structure of $\mathcal{T}_n$. 
    Then $\max \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \mathcal{O}_\mathbf{P}(1)$, i.e.:
    \[
        \limsup_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(\max \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n) \geq A\right)} \underset{A \rightarrow \infty}{\rightarrow} 0 \,.
    \]
\end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. 
Let $(Z_1,Z_2,\ldots)$ be i.i.d.\ random variables such that $\mathbf{P}{\left(Z_1=i\right)}=X_i^{(a)}$ for all $i$, and let 
\[
    t_M := \inf\{ t \geq 1 : \text{for all } j \in [M], \text{ there exists } s \leq t \text{ such that } Z_s= j \}
\] 
be the first time $t \geq 1$ for which $[M] \subseteq \{Z_1,\ldots,Z_t\}$. 
It is clear that $t_M<\infty$ almost surely. 
Hence, one can choose $Q \geq 1$ such that $\mathbf{P}{\left(t_M \leq Q\right)} \geq 1-\varepsilon$.

For $(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \in \mathbb{N}^Q$, let $T(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)$ be the discrete tree obtained from $(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)$ after the first $Q$ steps of the countable Foata--Fuchs construction (\Cref{def:countable foata}). 
That is, the vertices of $T(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)$ are all $v_i$ (where $v_i$ has label $i$) such that there exists $j \in [Q]$ with  $z_j=i$.
The root of $T(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)$ is $v_{z_1}$. For $2 \leq j \leq Q$ such that $z_j \neq \{z_1,\ldots,z_{j-1}\}$, $v_{z_j}$ is a child of $v_{z_{j-1}}$. 
Let $T_M(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \in \mathbb{T}_M^\le$ denote the subtree of $T(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)$ obtained by removing all vertices that have no descendant with a label in $[M]$.
For later use,
consider $\mathfrak{T} \in \mathbb{T}_M^{\geq}$ and an arbitrary $(z_1, \ldots, z_Q) \in \mathbb{N}^Q$ such that $T_M(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)=\mathfrak{T}$, if it exists. 

As in the construction of \Cref{def: M structure of discrete tree}, we label the vertices of $\mathcal{T}_n^{k_n,\mathrm{red}}$ according to the sizes of the associated components (with ties broken arbitrarily).
That is, we give the label $i$ to the vertex of $\mathcal{T}_n^{k_n,\mathrm{red}}$ with component $(\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n}$.
Conditionally given $\ell^{k_n}(\mathcal{T}_n) = \ell$, consider a vector $\mathbf{c} = (c_1, \dots, c_\ell)$ of positive integers summing to $\ell-1$. 
Then, conditionally on $\mathbf{c}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n):= \mathbf{c}$, we know by \Cref{lem:combinatorial formulas} (ii) that $\mathcal{T}_n^{k_n,\mathrm{red}}$ is distributed as a uniformly random tree with the prescribed out-degree sequence $(c_1,\ldots,c_\ell)$, where $c_i$ is the out-degree of the vertex labeled $i$.

Let $(x_1,\ldots,x_{\ell-1}) \in [\ell]^{\ell-1}_\mathbf{c}$ be the image of $\mathcal{T}_n^{k_n,\mathrm{red}} \in \mathbb{T}_{\ell,\mathbf{c}}$ by the Foata--Fuchs bijection (\Cref{thm:foata fuchs}).
Conditionally given $\mathbf{c}_n := \mathbf{c}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n)$, we know that $(x_1,\ldots,x_{\ell-1})$ is distributed uniformly on $[\ell]^{\ell-1}_{\mathbf{c}_n}$.
Then, using \eqref{eq:concentration ell}, we get under our coupling that $\ell^{k_n}(\mathcal{T}_n) \sim a\log k_n \overset{a.s.}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} \infty$, and also
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. (x_1,\ldots,x_Q)=(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \,\right|\, \mathbf{c}_n \right)} = \prod_{i=1}^Q \frac{c_{z_i}(\mathcal{T}_n)}{a \log k_n} (1+o_{a.s.}(1))
    \overset{a.s.}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} \prod_{i=1}^Q X^{(a)}_{z_i}.
\end{align*}
Now, recalling the construction of the dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$ from the sequence $X^{(a)}$ (Definition \ref{def: size-biased dendron}), we have
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \Big. (Z_1,\ldots,Z_Q)=(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)  \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)} =\prod_{i=1}^Q \mathbf{P}{\left(Z_i=z_i\right)}
    = \prod_{i=1}^{Q} X^{(a)}_{z_i}.
\end{align*}
Finally, observe that $\operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)$ equals $T_M(Z_1, \dots, Z_Q)$ for any $Q\ge1$ such that $Q \ge t_M$,
and likewise that $\operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n)$ equals $T_M(x_1, \dots, x_Q)$ for any $Q\ge1$ such that $[M] \subseteq \{ x_1, \dots, x_Q \}$.
Hence,
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\mathfrak{T} \,\right|\, \mathbf{c}_n \right)} 
&\geq \sum\limits_{\substack{(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \in \mathbb{N}^Q \\ T_M(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)=\mathfrak{T}}} \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. (x_1,\ldots,x_Q)=(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \,\right|\, \mathbf{c}_n \right)} \\
                                                                                                                          
&= \sum\limits_{\substack{(z_1,\ldots,z_Q) \in\mathbb{N}^Q \\ T_M(z_1,\ldots,z_Q)=\mathfrak{T}}}\prod_{i=1}^Q X_{z_i}^{(a)} (1+o_{\text{a.s.}}(1))\\
& \geq \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. \operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)} - \mathbf{P}{\left(t_M > Q\right)} + o(1)\\
& \geq \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. \operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)} -\varepsilon + o(1) \,.
\end{align*}
Letting $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$, we find that, for all $\mathfrak{T} \in \mathbb{T}^{\geq}_M$,
\begin{align*}
\liminf_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  \operatorname{St}_M^{(k_n)}(\mathcal{T}_n)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, \mathbf{c}_n \right)} \geq \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \Big. \operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)=\mathfrak{T}  \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)}
\end{align*}
almost surely, which concludes the proof of (i).
The proof of (ii) follows from the fact that, for any fixed $Q \geq 1$, $\mathbf{P}{\left(\max\limits_{1 \leq i \leq Q} Z_i \geq A\right)} \,\underset{A\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 0$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:same component}
Let $u_n$ be a uniform vertex in $\mathcal{T}_n$, and $u \in \mathcal{D}_a$ with law $\nu_a$. 
Then, for all $i \in [M]$, under the almost sure convergence \eqref{eq:kn},
\[
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. u_n \in (\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n} \,\right|\, \mathbf{n}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n) \right)} 
    \overset{a.s.}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 
    \mathbf{P}{\left(\left. \big. u=(v_{i},1) \,\right|\, X^{(a)} \right)} \,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
This is immediate by the convergence \eqref{eq:kn} of $\frac1n \mathbf{n}^\searrow(\mathcal{T}_n)$ towards $X^{(a)}$, the fact that the coordinates of $X^{(a)}$ are a.s.\ distinct, and the construction of the dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:spanned subtree}
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     
Assume (A1) and (A2) for sufficiently large $n$. 
Then, conditionally on these events,
\begin{align*}
    \frac{1}{\log n} \left( \mathrm{dist}_{\mathcal{T}_n}(w_r^{(n)},w_s^{(n)}) \right)_{1 \leq r,s \leq p}
    \overset{\mathbf{P}}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,}
    \left( 2+\mathrm{dist}_{\mathfrak{T}}(v_{i_r},v_{i_s}) \right)_{1 \leq r,s \leq p} \,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem: distances in a dendron}
Consider the dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$. 
Let $w_1, \dots, w_p$ be as in (B1), 
                                                                   
and assume that (B2) holds. 
Then,
\begin{align*}
    \left( d_a(w_{r},w_{s}) \right)_{1 \leq r,s \leq p} = \left(\big. 2+\mathrm{dist}_{\mathfrak{T}}(v_{i_r},v_{i_s})\right)_{1 \leq r,s \leq p} \,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Consider $M,Q \geq 1$ such that $E_{M,Q}$ holds with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$. 
Under the event $E_{M,Q}$, there are only finitely many possible trees $\mathfrak{T}$ and finitely many allocations $(i_1, \ldots, i_p)$ such that $\mathbf{P}{\left(\left.  \operatorname{St}_M(\mathcal{D}_a)=\mathfrak{T} \, ; \, w_r = (v_{i_r},1) \text{ for all } r \in [p]  \,\right|\,  E_{M,Q} \right)}>0$. 
Fix any such $(\mathfrak{T},i_1,\ldots,i_p)$. 
Using the coupling of \Cref{sssec:coupling} along with Lemmas \ref{lem:proba of a structure} and \ref{lem:same component}, our result follows from Proposition \ref{prop:spanned subtree} and Lemma \ref{lem: distances in a dendron}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
First, we claim that we only need to prove our result under the additional assumptions that
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(C1)] $(n)_Q^{k_n} \geq n/\log n$; 
    \item[(C2)] $N^{(k_n)}\big( (\mathcal{T}_n)_i^{k_n} \big) = o\big( (n)_i^{k_n} \big)$ for all $i \in [Q]$;
    \item[(C3)] There is no descent edge between two vertices of labels $\leq k_n$. 
\end{itemize}
Indeed, these three events hold with high probability as $n \rightarrow \infty$, using \Cref{lem:no small descent,lem:lemmerelou5bis,lem:lemmerelou4bis}.

Observe that, conditionally on assumptions (A1) and (A2), for all $r \in [p]$ independently, $w_r^{(n)}$ is a uniform vertex of $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{i_r}^{k_n}$.
Furthermore, the following holds for all $j\in[Q]$ such that $(\mathcal{T}_n)_j^{k_n}$ is not the root component of $\mathcal{T}_n$.
Let $\rho_j$ denote the root of $(\mathcal{T}_n)_j^{k_n}$, and $x_j$ its parent in $\mathcal{T}_n$.
Then, $x_j$ is a uniformly random vertex of $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{p(j)}^{k_n} \setminus t_{p(j)}^{k_n}$, where $p(j) \in \mathcal{T}_n^{k_n,\mathrm{red}}$ is such that $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{p(j)}^{k_n}$ is the component containing $x_j$. 
Recall that $t_{p(j)}^{k_n}$ is the subtree of $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{p(j)}^{k_n}$ consisting of labels $\le k_n$.

Hence, by (C2), with probability $1-o(1)$, $w_r^{(n)} \in T^\circ_{i_r}$, where $T^\circ_{i_r} := \big( V^{(k_n)}\big( (\mathcal{T}_n)_{i_r}^{k_n} \big) \big)^c$ is the set of vertices in $(\mathcal{T}_n)_{i_r}^{k_n}$ such that all descent endpoints in their ancestral line have labels $\leq k_n$. 
In addition, conditionally on that event, $w_r^{(n)}$ is distributed as a uniform vertex of $T^\circ_{i_r}$. 
Furthermore, with probability $1-o(1)$ for all valid $j\in[Q]$, the vertex $x_j$ is an element of $T^\circ_{p(j)}$ and, conditionally on this event, is a uniform vertex of $T^\circ_{p(j)} \setminus t_{p(j)}^{k_n}$. 

By (C1) and (C3), for all $i \in [Q]$, $(n)_i^{k_n} \geq n/\log n$ and $T^\circ_i$, conditionally on its size, is distributed as a random recursive tree of that size. 
We finally use the following classical result (see, e.g., \cite{Panholzer_2004}): let $T_m$ be a random recursive tree with $m$ vertices, let $u,v$ be i.i.d.\ uniform vertices in $T_m$, $d(u,v)$ their graph distance in $T_m$, and $h(u)$ the height of the vertex $u$. Then, as $m \rightarrow \infty$, $d(u,v) = 2\log(m)(1+o_{\textbf{P}}(1))$ and $h(u)=\log(m)(1+o_{\textbf{P}}(1))$.

This immediately implies that, conditionally on (A1), (A2), (C1), (C2) and (C3),
\begin{itemize}
    \item for all $r \in [p]$, $\mathrm{dist}_n(w_r^{(n)},x_{i_r})=\log(n) (1+o_\mathbf{P}(1))$;
    \item for all $j$ such that $j$ is the label of a non-root vertex of $\mathfrak{T}$, $\mathrm{dist}_n(x_j, x_{p(j)})=\log(n)(1+o_\mathbf{P}(1))$;
    \item for all labels $j_1,j_2$ of non-root vertices of $\mathfrak{T}$ such that $p(j_1)=p(j_2)$, $\mathrm{dist}_n(x_{j_1},x_{j_2})=2\log(n) (1+o_\mathbf{P}(1))$.
\end{itemize}
The result follows from the tree structure of $\mathfrak{T}$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem: asymptotic first term and variance for PD}
    For $a>0$, let $\big( X_{a,1} \ge X_{a,2} \ge \dots \big)$ be a $\mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)}$ random variable and $\sigma_a^2 := \sum_{i\ge1} X_{a,i}^2$.
    Then,
    \begin{equation*}
        a X_{a,1}^2 \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 0
        \quad\text{and}\quad
        a\sigma_a^2 \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 1 \,.
    \end{equation*}
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
The distribution of $X_{a,1}$ is given, e.g., in \cite{Arratia_Barbour_Tavare_2003}.
    By \cite[Eq.\ (4.101)]{Arratia_Barbour_Tavare_2003}, we have
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}{\left[X_{a,1}^2\right]}
        = \frac{1}{a+1} \int_0^\infty x e^{-x-aE_1(x)} \mathrm dx,
        \quad\text{where}\quad
        E_1(x) := \int_x^\infty y^{-1} e^{-y} \mathrm dy \,.
    \end{equation*}
    It is straightforward, by dominated convergence, to see that $a \cdot \mathbb{E}{\left[X_{a,1}^2\right]} \to 0$ as $a\to\infty$.
    Therefore, $a X_{a,1}^2 \to 0$ in probability as $a\to\infty$.
    For the second statement, we rely on a simple distributional equation for $\sigma_a^2$.
    Indeed, let $B_a$ denote a $\mathrm{Beta}{\left(1,a\right)}$ random variable, independent of $\sigma_a^2$.
    Then, we have the following equality in distribution:
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma_a^2 \overset{(d)}{=} B_a^2 + (1-B_a)^2 \sigma_a^2 \,.
    \end{equation*}
    From this we deduce
    \[
        \mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^2\right]} = \frac{2}{(1+a)(2+a)} + \frac{a}{2+a} \mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^2\right]}\,,
    \]
    and so $\mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^2\right]} = \frac{1}{1+a}$.
    Squaring the distributional equality further gives
    \[
        \mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^4\right]} = \frac{24}{(1+a)(2+a)(3+a)(4+a)}
        + \frac{4a}{(2+a)(3+a)(4+a)} \mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^2\right]} + \frac{a}{4+a} \mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^4\right]}\,,
    \]
    and so $\mathbb{E}{\left[\sigma_a^4\right]} = \frac{6+a}{(1+a)(2+a)(3+a)}$.
    It follows that $\mathrm{Var}{\left[\sigma_a^2\right]} = \frac{2a}{(1+a)^2(2+a)(3+a)} = o(1/a^2)$ and that $a\sigma_a^2 \,\underset{}{\longrightarrow}\, 1$ in probability as $a\to\infty$ by the Bienaymé--Chebyshev inequality.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
The proof of (i) is now immediate, as $X^{(a)} \,\underset{a\to0}{\longrightarrow}\, (1,0,\ldots)$ in distribution with respect to the $\ell_\infty$ norm on $\Delta$. 
So we turn to the proof of (ii).
    For $a>0$, let ${\mathfrak{p}}_a$ be the random probability measure on $\mathbb{N}$ given by
    \[
        {\mathfrak{p}}_a(i) = X_{a,i} \text{ for all } i\ge1,
        \quad\text{where}\quad
        \big( X_{a,1} \ge X_{a,2} \ge \dots \big) \sim \mathrm{PD}{\left(0,a\right)} \,.
    \]
    Then, the base tree $T_a^{\operatorname{disc}}$ of our dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$ is a ${\mathfrak{p}}_a$-tree in the sense of \cite{Camarri_Pitman_2000,Blanc-Renaudie_2025}.
    We wish to use \cite[Theorem~6.2]{Blanc-Renaudie_2025}, which gives a criterium for the Gromov--Prokhorov convergence of p-trees toward inhomogeneous CRTs (in our case, the regular CRT).
    See also the discussion in \cite[Section~5]{Camarri_Pitman_2000}.
    In our case, it suffices to check that
    \begin{equation*}
        X_{a,1} / \sigma_a \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} 0
    \end{equation*}
    where $\sigma_a = \sqrt{\sum_{i\ge1} X_{a,i}^2}$.
    This is a direct consequence of \Cref{lem: asymptotic first term and variance for PD}.
    Therefore, we get that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \left( T_a^{\operatorname{disc}} , {\sigma_a} \cdot d_a , {\mathfrak{p}}_a \right)
        \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,}
        \left( \mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e} , \mathrm{dist}, \mu \right)
    \end{equation*}
    with respect to the Gromov--Prokhorov topology.
    In this convergence statement, the right-hand side is the Brownian CRT.
    The left-hand side is the discrete base tree of the dendron $\mathcal{D}_a$ with distances renormalized by $\sigma_a$ and measure ${\mathfrak{p}}_a = \sum_{i\ge1} X_{a,i} \delta_{v_i}$ on its vertices.
    Since $\sigma_a \sim 1/\sqrt{a}$ in probability as $a\to\infty$ by \Cref{lem: asymptotic first term and variance for PD}, it is straightforward to see that the Prokhorov distance between the measures ${\mathfrak{p}}_a$ on ${\sigma_a} \cdot T_a^{\operatorname{disc}}$ and $\nu_a$ on $\sigma_a \cdot \mathcal{D}_a$ goes to $0$ in probability, and thus
    \begin{equation*}
        \left( \mathcal{D}_a , \frac{1}{\sqrt a} \cdot d_a , \nu_a \right)
        \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{a\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,}
        \left( \mathcal{T}_\mathbf{e} , \mathrm{dist}, \mu \right)
    \end{equation*}
    follows.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:stochastic bound for size subtree}
For any given $q\in(0,1]$, we have
\begin{align*}
    \limsup_{n \rightarrow \infty} \sup_{1 \leq k \leq n} \mathbf{P}{\left(|\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}(k)| \geq M\right)} \,\underset{M\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 0. 
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(i)] For all $q \in (0,1]$,
        \begin{align*}
            \limsup_{n \rightarrow \infty} \mathbf{P}{\left(|\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}(1)| \geq M\right)} \,\underset{M\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, 0.
        \end{align*}
    \item[(ii)] For all $n \geq 1$, all $q \in (0,1]$, and all $k \leq n-1$, we have $\mathbf{P}{\left(|\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}(k)| \geq M\right)} \geq \mathbf{P}{\left(|\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}(k+1)| \geq M\right)} .$
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Let us prove (i). Define, for $d \geq 0$ and $n > M \geq 1$, 
    \begin{align*}
        B_M(x,q) := \sum_{n \geq 1, d \geq 0}\frac{
                   
        {\left|\mathbb{V}_{n,d,1,M,1}\right|}
        }{n!} x^n q^d,
    \end{align*}
    where we recall that $\mathbb{V}_{n,d,1,M,1}$ is the set of trees $T$ with $d$ descents, $n$ vertices and such that $|T(1)|=M$ (counting the vertex $v_1$ labeled $1$ itself). 
    When $n>M$, there is necessarily a descent leading to $v_1$ in such trees, and thus $v_1$ is not the root of the tree. 
    We immediately get that, for any fixed $n>M \geq 1$,
    \begin{align*}
    [x^n] B_M(x,q) = q \frac{n-M}{n} [x^{n-M}] A(x,q) [x^{M-1}] e^{A(x,q)},
    \end{align*}
    so that, for fixed $M\ge1$ and as $n\to\infty$,
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(|\mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}(1)|=M\right)} 
        = q \frac{n-M}{n} [x^{M-1}] e^{A(x,q)} \frac{[x^{n-M}]A(x,q)}{[x^n] A(x,q)}
        \sim q x_0^M [x^{M-1}] e^{A(x,q)}                                                       
    \end{align*}
    by \eqref{eq: asymptotic coefficient A for fixed q}, where $x_0:=q^{\frac{q}{1-q}}$ is the dominant singularity of $A(x,q)$. 
    Using the fact that 
    \begin{align*}
        \sum_{M \geq 1} q x_0^M [x^{M-1}]e^{A(x,q)}=qx_0e^{A(x_0,q)}=1
    \end{align*}
    by \eqref{eq: asymptotic A close to singularity for fixed q}, we get the result.
    
     We now prove (ii). Fix $k \leq n-1$. 
     Let $E_1 \subset \mathbb{T}_n$ be the set of trees where $v_k$ and $v_{k+1}$ are not connected by an edge, $E_2$ the set of trees where $v_k$ is a child of $v_{k+1}$ and $E_3$ the set of trees where $v_{k+1}$ is a child of $v_k$.
     We consider a random transformation $T \mapsto \hat{T}$ on $\mathbb{T}_n$. 
     For $T \in \mathbb{T}_n$, define $T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}$ as the tree in which the labels of the vertices labeled $k$ and $k+1$ have been swapped (leaving the rest of the tree and the tree structure unchanged). 
     Now, define $\hat{T}$ as follows:
     \begin{itemize}
         \item If $T \in E_1$, then $\hat{T}$ is set to $T$ with probability $\frac12$, and it is set to $T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}$ with probability $\frac12$;
         \item If $T \in E_2$, then $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{T}=T\right)}=\frac{q}{q+1}$ and $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{T}=T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}\right)}=\frac{1}{q+1}$;
         \item If $T \in E_3$, then $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{T}=T\right)}=\frac{1}{q+1}$ and $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{T}=T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}\right)}=\frac{q}{q+1}$.
     \end{itemize}
     The map $T \mapsto \hat{T}$ preserves the distribution of $\mathcal{T}_n := \mathcal{T}_n^{(q)}$. 
     Indeed, we have the following cases:
     \begin{itemize}
         \item If $T \in E_1$, then $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}=T\right)}=\frac{1}{2} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T\right)}+\frac{1}{2} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}\right)} = \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T\right)}$ since $T$ has as many descents as $T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}$.
         \item If $T \in E_2$, then $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}=T\right)}=\frac{q}{q+1} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T\right)}+\frac{q}{q+1} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}\right)} = \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T\right)}$ since $T$ has one more descent than $T^{k \leftrightarrow k+1}$.
         \item If $T \in E_3$, we get analogously that $\mathbf{P}{\left(\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}=T\right)}=\mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n=T\right)}$.
     \end{itemize}
Now fix $M \geq 1$. We have
\begin{align*}
&\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_1\right)} 
\\&= \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}(k)\right|}, \hat{\mathcal{T}_n} \in E_1\right)}\\
&= \frac12 \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_1\right)} + \frac12 \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_1\right)},
\end{align*}
so that $\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_1\right)}=\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_1\right)}$.

On the other hand,
\begin{align*}
&\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_2\right)} 
\\&= \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\hat{\mathcal{T}_n}(k)\right|}, \hat{\mathcal{T}_n} \in E_2\right)}\\
&= \frac{q}{q+1} \left[\Big. \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_2\right)} + \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_3\right)} \right] \,,
\end{align*}
so that $\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_2\right)} = q \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_3\right)}$.

Finally, we have $\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_3\right)} = \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}, \mathcal{T}_n \in E_2\right)}=0$.
Using the fact that 
\[ 
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M\right)} - \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M\right)} = \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|}\right)} - \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k)\right|} \geq M > {\left|\mathcal{T}_n(k+1)\right|}\right)}
\]
together with the previous inequalities, we get the result.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:distribution of the small tree}
Fix $i,m$ with $m \geq i \geq 1$, and any function $g: \mathbb{N} \rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ such that $g(n) \rightarrow \infty$ as $n \rightarrow \infty$. As $n \rightarrow \infty$, uniformly for all $r \in \llbracket g(n), n-g(n) \rrbracket$, we have
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(r)\right|}=m, \operatorname{rk}(r)=i, {\rm par}(r) < r\right)} \underset{n \rightarrow \infty}{\sim} \frac{r/n}{r/n+q(1-r/n)} f_{m,i}^{(q)}\left(\frac{r}{n}\right),
\end{align*}
and 
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(r)\right|}=m, \operatorname{rk}(r)=i, {\rm par}(r) > r\right)} \underset{n \rightarrow \infty}{\sim} \frac{q(1-r/n)}{r/n+q(1-r/n)}f_{m,i}^{(q)}\left(\frac{r}{n}\right).
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:sums at 1}
Fix $i,m$ with $m \geq i \geq 1$, and any function $g: \mathbb{N} \rightarrow \mathbb{N}$ such that $g(n) \rightarrow \infty$ as $n \rightarrow \infty$. As $n \rightarrow \infty$, uniformly for all $r \in \llbracket g(n), n-g(n) \rrbracket$, we have
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(i)]  $\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(r)\right|}=m, \operatorname{rk}(r)=i\right)} \underset{n \rightarrow \infty}{\sim} f_{m,i}^{(q)}\left(\frac{r}{n}\right)$;
    \item[(ii)] $\lim\limits_{M \rightarrow \infty}\sum\limits_{m=1}^M\sum\limits_{i=1}^m f_{m,i}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r}{n} \right) = 1 + o(1)$.
    \item[(iii)] For any given $\chi\in[0,1]$, $\sum_{m\ge i\ge 1} f_{m,i}^{(q)}(\chi) = 1$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
This follows immediately from Proposition \ref{prop:stochastic bound for size subtree} and Lemma \ref{lem:distribution of the small tree}.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Let $\mathbb{V}^>_{n,d,r,m,i}$ (resp., $\mathbb{V}^<_{n,d,r,m,i}$) denote the subset of $\mathbb{V}_{n,d,r,m,i}$ of trees in which ${\rm par}(r)>r$ (resp., ${\rm par}(r)<r$).
We have, for all $n,r,m$,
\begin{align*}
\sum_{d \geq 0} |\mathbb{V}^<_{n,d,r,m,i}| q^d = (r-i)  \binom{r-1}{i-1} \binom{n-r}{m-i} \sum_{d' \geq 0} |\mathbb{T}_{n-m,d'}| q^{d'} \sum_{d_0 \geq 0} |\mathbb{U}_{m,d_0,i}|q^{d_0} \,.
\end{align*}
Indeed, the first factor comes from the choice of the parent of $v_r$, and the binomial terms account for the choice of the labels of the vertices in the subtree grafted at $v_r$. 
In particular, for fixed $i$ and $m$, and uniformly for $g(n) \leq r \leq n-g(n)$, we have
\begin{align*}
\sum_{d \geq 0} \frac{|\mathbb{V}^<_{n,d,r,m,i}|}{n!} q^d &= (1+o(1))\frac{(n-m)!}{n!} r \frac{r^{i-1}}{(i-1)!} \frac{(n-r)^{m-i}}{(m-i)!} \sum_{d' \geq 0} \frac{|\mathbb{T}_{n-m,d'}|}{(n-m)!} q^{d'} \sum_{d_0 \geq 0} |\mathbb{U}_{m,d_0,i}|q^{d_0} \\
&= (1+o(1)) \frac{r}{n}  \frac{\left( \frac{r}{n} \right)^{i-1}}{(i-1)!} \frac{\left( 1 - \frac{r}{n} \right)^{m-i}}{(m-i)!} \sum_{d' \geq 0} \frac{|\mathbb{T}_{n-m,d'}|}{(n-m)!} q^{d'} \sum_{d_0 \geq 0} |\mathbb{U}_{m,d_0,i}|q^{d_0}.
\end{align*}
This entails, using \eqref{eq: asymptotic coefficient A for fixed q} for the last step,
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left({\left|\mathcal{T}_n(r)\right|}=m, \operatorname{rk}(r)=i, {\rm par}(r)<r\right)} &=\mathbf{P}{\left(\mathcal{T}_n \in \bigcup_{d \geq 0}\mathbb{V}^<_{n,d,r,m,i}\right)}\\
&= (1+o(1)) \frac{r}{n} \frac{\left( \frac{r}{n} \right)^{i-1}}{(i-1)!} \frac{\left( 1 - \frac{r}{n} \right)^{m-i}}{(m-i)!} \sum_{d_0 \geq 0} |\mathbb{U}_{m,d_0,i}|q^{d_0} \frac{[x^{n-m}] A(x,q)}{[x^n]A(x,q)}\\
&=(1+o(1)) x_0^m\frac{r}{n} \frac{\left( \frac{r}{n} \right)^{i-1}}{(i-1)!} \frac{\left( 1 - \frac{r}{n} \right)^{m-i}}{(m-i)!} \sum_{d_0 \geq 0} |\mathbb{U}_{m,d_0,i}|q^{d_0}.
\end{align*}
The second part of Lemma \ref{lem:distribution of the small tree} is obtained in an analogous way.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:ancestral line local limit fixed q}
Fix $p \geq 0$, and fix $p+1$ pairs of integers $(m_j,i_j) \in \mathbb{N}^2$ such that $m_j \geq i_j \geq 1$ for $0 \leq j \leq p$. 
Then, as $n \rightarrow \infty$, uniformly for all distinct $r_0,\ldots,r_p \in \llbracket 1,n \rrbracket$ such that $\sqrt{n} \leq r_j \leq n-\sqrt{n}$ for all $0 \leq j \leq p$, 
we have
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\operatorname{AL}_p(r_0) = ((r_j,m_j,i_j))_{0 \leq j \leq p}\right)} 
\:\underset{n \rightarrow\infty}{\sim}\:
\prod_{j=0}^p f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r_j}{n} \right) \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \frac{\mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} < r_j} + q \mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} > r_j}}{r_j + q(n-r_j)}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Consider $\textbf{a} := ((r_j,m_j,i_j))_{0 \leq j \leq p}$ and let $M_p := \sum_{j=0}^p m_j$.
Let $\mathbb{V}_{n,d,\textbf{a}}$ denote the set of trees with $n$ labeled vertices and $d$ descents such that $\operatorname{AL}_p(r_0)=\textbf{a}$. We have, in the same way as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:distribution of the small tree},
\begin{multline*}
\sum_{d \geq 0} {\left|\mathbb{V}_{n,d,\textbf{a}}\right|} q^d 
\\= (1+o(1)) \left[ r_p+q(n-r_p) \right] 
\left( \prod_{j=0}^p \frac{r_j^{i_j-1}}{(i_j-1)!} \frac{(n-r_j)^{m_j-i_j}}{(m_j-i_j)!} \sum_{d_j \geq 0} {\left|\mathbb{U}_{m_j,d_j,i_j}\right|}q^{d_j} \right)
\sum_{d' \geq 0} |\mathbb{T}_{n-M_p,d'}| q^{d'}  q^{d(\sigma_p)},
\end{multline*}
where $d(\sigma_p):= {\left|\{ j \in [p], r_j<r_{j-1}\}\right|}$ is the number of descents in the permutation induced by $\{r_p,r_{p-1},\ldots,r_0\}$.
This can be rewritten as follows:
\begin{align*}
&\sum_{d \geq 0} \frac{{\left|\mathbb{V}_{n,d,\textbf{a}}\right|}}{n!} q^d 
\\&= (1+o(1)) n^{-p} \left[ \frac{r_p}{n} + q \left( 1-\frac{r_p}{n} \right) \right] 
\left( \prod_{j=0}^p \frac{\left(\frac{r_j}{n}\right)^{i_j-1}}{(i_j-1)!} \frac{\left(1-\frac{r_j}{n}\right)^{m_j-i_j}}{(m_j-i_j)!} \sum_{d_j \geq 0} {\left|\mathbb{U}_{m_j,d_j,i_j}\right|}q^{d_j} \right)
[x^{n-M_p}]A(x,q) q^{d(\sigma_p)}
\\&= (1+o(1)) n^{-p} 
\left( \prod_{j=0}^p x_0^{-m_j} f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r_j}{n} \right) \right)
\left( \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \left( \frac{r_j}{n} + q\left( 1-\frac{r_j}{n}\right) \right)^{-1} \right)
[x^{n-M_p}]A(x,q) q^{d(\sigma_p)} \,.
\end{align*}
Therefore, we get
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(\big. \operatorname{AL}_p(r_0) = ((r_j,m_j,i_j))_{0 \leq j \leq p}\right)} &= \frac{1}{[x^n]A(x,q)} \sum_{d \geq 0} \frac{{\left|\mathbb{V}_{n,d,\textbf{a}}\right|}}{n!} q^d\\
&= (1+o(1)) n^{-p} \prod_{j=0}^p f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r_j}{n} \right) \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \left( \frac{r_j}{n} + q\left( 1-\frac{r_j}{n}\right) \right)^{-1} q^{d(\sigma_p)}\\
&= (1+o(1)) \prod_{j=0}^p f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r_j}{n} \right) \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \frac{\mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} < r_j} + q \mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} > r_j}}{r_j + q(n-r_j)}
\end{align*}
as announced.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Fix $p \geq 0$. 
Let $R_0 := R^{(n)}_0$ be a uniformly random integer in $[n]$, and let $\operatorname{AL}_p(R_0) := (R_j,M_j,I_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p}$ be its $p$-ancestral line (with $(R_j,M_j,I_j)=(0,0,0)$ if $h(v_{R_0})<j$). 
By our \Cref{prop: Rayleigh limit of typical depth when q fixed} (or, alternatively, by \cite[Proposition 24]{Thevenin_Wagner_2023} and Markov's inequality), we know that $\mathbf{P}{\left(h(v_{R_0}) \leq p\right)} = o(1)$, where $h(v_{R_0})$ denotes the distance in $\mathcal{T}_n$ between the root and the vertex labeled $R_0$.

Let $F: ([0,1] \times \mathbb{N} \times \mathbb{N})^{p+1} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}$ be bounded and continuous. 
By \Cref{prop:ancestral line local limit fixed q} and dominated convergence, we have
\begin{align*}
\mathbb{E}&\left[ F\left(\left(\frac{R_j}{n},M_j,I_j\right)_{0 \leq j \leq p}\right) \right]\\ 
& = o(1)+\sum_{(r_j,m_j,i_j) \in ([n] \times \mathbb{N}^2)^{p+1}} F\big( (r_j/n,m_j,i_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p} \big) \mathbf{P}{\left(R_0=r_0\right)} \mathbf{P}{\left(\operatorname{AL}_p(r_0) := ((r_j,m_j,i_j))_{0 \leq j \leq p}\right)}\\
& = o(1) + \frac{1}{n}\sum_{(r_j,m_j,i_j) \in ([n]\times \mathbb{N}^2)^{p+1}} F\big( (r_j/n,m_j,i_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p} \big)\prod_{j=0}^p f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}\left( \frac{r_j}{n} \right) \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \frac{\mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} < r_j} + q \mathbbm{1}_{r_{j+1} > r_j}}{r_j + q(n-r_j)}\\
& \,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\, \int_{[0,1]^{p+1}} \sum_{(m_j,i_j) \in \mathbb{N}^{2p+2}} F(x_j,m_j,i_j) \prod_{j=0}^p f_{m_j,i_j}^{(q)}(x_j) \prod_{j=0}^{p-1} \frac{\mathbbm{1}_{x_{j+1}<x_j}+q\mathbbm{1}_{x_{j+1}>x_j}}{x_j+q(1-x_j)} \prod_{j=0}^p \mathrm dx_j\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[F\left( \left(X_j,\mathcal{M}_j,\mathcal{I}_j\right)_{0 \leq j \leq p} \right)\right]
\end{align*}
In the last step, $(X_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p}$ is distributed like the Markov chain of Definition \ref{def:t etoile q} and $(\mathcal{M}_j,\mathcal{I}_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p}$ are independent pairs conditionally on $(X_j)_{0 \leq j \leq p}$, with $(\mathcal{M}_j,\mathcal{I}_j)$ being distributed as $Y_{q,X_j}$ (see Definition \ref{def:grafted trees}). 
Finally, observe that clearly, for all $n$, for all $r \in [n]$, and all $(M,i) \in \mathbb{N}^2$, conditionally on $|\mathcal{T}_n(r)|=M$ and $\operatorname{rk}(r)=i$, the tree $\mathcal{T}_n(r)$ is distributed as $\mathcal{T}_{M,i}^{(q)}$. 
This completes our proof.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{def:local limit dadedzi wagner}
The local limit $(\mathcal{T}_*^\mathrm{rec}, \rho_*^\mathrm{rec})$ of a random recursive tree can be constructed as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Start with an infinite one-ended path of vertices $v_0,v_1,v_2,\ldots$.
    \item Define positive integer random variables $Z_0,Z_1,Z_2,\ldots$ such that $\mathbf{P}{\left(Z_0=k\right)}=\frac{1}{k(k+1)}$ for $k \geq 1$ and, for all $r \geq 1$, given $Z_0,Z_1,\ldots,Z_{r-1}$, for all $k \geq 1$,
    \begin{align*}
        \mathbf{P}{\left(Z_r=k\right)} =\frac{Y_{r-1}+1}{(k+Y_{r-1})(k+Y_{r-1}+1)},
    \end{align*}
    where $Y_{r-1} := Z_0+\ldots+Z_{r-1}$.
    \item For every $i \geq 0$, graft a random recursive tree onto $v_i$ that has $Z_i$ vertices ($v_i$ included). All these trees are taken to be independent.
    Finally, set $\rho_*^\mathrm{rec} = v_0$.
\end{itemize}
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:local limit when q_n goes to 0}
Let $(q_n)_{n \geq 1}$ be a sequence of elements of $[0,1]$ such that $q_n \rightarrow 0$ as $n \rightarrow \infty$. 
Then, in the sense of Definition \ref{def:bs local limit}, we have the following convergence in distribution:
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{T}_n^{(q_n)} \overset{(d)}{\,\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\,} (\mathcal{T}_*^\mathrm{rec} , \rho_*^\mathrm{rec}) \,.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:definitions of local limits agree}
The tree $(\mathcal{T}_*^{(0)},\rho_*^{(0)})$ obtained from Definition~\ref{def:t etoile q} with $q_*=0$ has the same distribution as the local limit $(\mathcal{T}_*^\mathrm{red}, \rho_*^\mathrm{rec})$ of random recursive trees from Definition~\ref{def:local limit dadedzi wagner}.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Recall from Remark~\ref{rk:kernels} that the tree in Definition~\ref{def:t etoile q} is obtained as in Definition~\ref{def:local limit dadedzi wagner}, by grafting independent size-conditioned random recursive trees onto $v_0,v_1,\ldots$. 
Their sizes are determined as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item let $X_0 \sim \mathrm{Unif}{\left([0,1]\right)}$;
    \item For $r \geq 1$, conditionally on $X_{r-1}$, let $X_r \sim \mathrm{Unif}{\left([0,X_{r-1}]\right)}$;
    \item For $i \geq 0$, conditionally on $(X_0,X_1,\ldots,X_i)$, the tree grafted at $v_i$ has size $G_i \sim \mathrm{Geom}(X_i)$.
\end{itemize}
Hence, we only need to prove that, for any $r \geq 0$,
\begin{equation}
(G_0,\ldots,G_r) \sim (Z_0,\ldots,Z_r),
\end{equation}
where the sequence $(Z_i)_{i \geq 0}$ is as in Definition~\ref{def:local limit dadedzi wagner}.
To this end, fix $k_0, \ldots, k_r \in \mathbb{N}_0$. It is proved in \cite[Proof of Theorem A.1]{Dadedzi_Wagner_2024} that
\begin{align*}
    \mathbf{P}{\left((Z_0,\ldots,Z_r)=(k_0,\ldots,k_r)\right)} = \frac{1}{K_0 K_1 \cdots K_r(K_r+1)},
\end{align*}
where $K_j := \sum_{i=0}^j k_i$ for $0 \leq j \leq r$.
Let us show that this agrees with $\mathbf{P}{\left(E_r\right)}$, where $E_r$ is the event $\{(G_0,\ldots,G_r)=(k_0,\ldots,k_r)\}$. 
By definition, we can write
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(E_r\right)}&= \int_0^1 \mathrm dx_0 \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathrm{Geom}(x_0)=k_0\right)} 
\int_0^{x_0} \frac{\mathrm dx_1}{x_0} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathrm{Geom}(x_1)=k_1\right)}
\ldots
\int_0^{x_{r-1}} \frac{\mathrm dx_r}{x_{r-1}} \mathbf{P}{\left(\mathrm{Geom}(x_r)=k_r\right)} \\
&= \int_0^1 \mathrm dx_0 (1-x_0)^{k_0-1} x_0 
\int_0^{x_0} \mathrm dx_1 \frac{(1-x_1)^{k_1-1} x_1}{x_0} 
\ldots
\int_0^{x_{r-1}} \mathrm dx_r \frac{(1-x_r)^{k_r-1} x_r}{x_{r-1}} \\
&= \int_0^1 \mathrm dx_0 (1-x_0)^{k_0-1} 
\int_0^{x_0} \mathrm dx_1 (1-x_1)^{k_1-1} 
\ldots 
\int_0^{x_{r-1}} \mathrm dx_r (1-x_r)^{k_r-1} x_r \\
&= \int_0^1 \mathrm dx_r\, x_r(1-x_r)^{k_r-1}  
\int_{x_r}^1 \mathrm dx_{r-1} (1-x_{r-1})^{k_{r-1}-1}  
\ldots 
\int_{x_1}^1 \mathrm dx_0 (1-x_0)^{k_0-1} \,.
\end{align*}
The last integral is equal to $\int_0^{1-x_1} y_0^{k_0-1} \mathrm dy_0 = \frac{(1-x_1)^{k_0}}{k_0}$,
so we have
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(E_r\right)}
&=
\frac{1}{k_0} \int_0^1 \mathrm dx_r\, x_r(1-x_r)^{k_r-1} 
\ldots 
\int_{x_2}^1 \mathrm dx_1 (1-x_1)^{k_1+k_0-1} \,.
\end{align*}
By immediate induction, we get
\begin{align*}
\mathbf{P}{\left(G_0=k_0,\ldots,G_r=k_r\right)}
&= \frac{1}{k_0(k_0+k_1) \cdots (k_0+\ldots+k_{r-1})} \int_0^1 x_r(1-x_r)^{\sum_{i=0}^r k_i-1} \mathrm dx_r \\
&= \frac{1}{K_0 K_1 \ldots K_r (K_r+1)}.
\end{align*}
This completes the proof, as the distribution of $(G_0,\ldots,G_r)$ characterizes the tree $\mathcal{T}_*^{(0)}$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem: ratio of partition functions when q to 0}
    Let $(q_n)_{n\ge1}$ be a sequence of parameters in $[0,1]$ such that $q_n\to0$.
    Then
    \[
    \frac{Z_{n-1,q_n}}{Z_{n,q_n}} \sim \frac1n 
    \]
    as $n\to\infty$.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
